## Dedication

THIS BOOK is an offering at the lotus feet of my beloved spiritual master His Divine Grace A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami Prabhupāda, affectionately known as Śrīla Prabhupāda. My memories of Śrīla Prabhupāda’s one and only visit to South Africa are very dear to me and I sincerely wish to share them with everyone.

He graced us with his compassion even though we were all immersed in the darkness of ignorance. He illuminated us with spiritual knowledge and love of God, giving us the opportunity to go back home, back to Godhead.

It is out of love and gratitude that I now, in all humility, dedicate this book, *Mission in the Service of His Divine Grace*, to my dear Guru Mahārāja, Śrīla Prabhupāda.

**Riddha Dāsa Adhikārī**

## Foreword

In telling the story of Śrīla Prabhupāda’s achievements and travels during the eleven years that he circled the world, each individual day would make a story in itself. Although Śrīla Prabhupāda only visited South Africa once, the actual visit was preceded by a background of intrigue, owing to the nature of the former South African government. Riddha dāsa Adhikārī has gone to great lengths with this labour of love to research and present the unique circumstances surrounding Śrīla Prabhupāda’s visit to the southern tip of Africa.

The full impact of that visit can be seen today, now that Śrīla Prabhupāda’s movement is so successful in South Africa. In establishing the s*aṇkīrtana* movement worldwide, Śri Caitanya Mahāprabhu will eventually reach every town and village in the world.

This book may well serve as a reference for South African devotees for many generations to come. This could prove to be an important insight into Śrīla Prabhupāda’s role in the history of South Africa itself. It not only highlights Śrīla Prabhupāda in South Africa, but also those supporters and disciples who implemented every aspect of his instructions. No one can fail to be impressed by the contribution of these individuals.

Riddha dāsa is uniquely placed to write this book, having been instrumental in the very foundation of ISKCON in South Africa. Though he continued to serve elsewhere in the world, he retained a valuable collection of photographs and personal contacts, together with his recollections, of the early founding days in South Africa. In writing this book, he has rendered a tremendous service to the thousands of *vaiṣṇava* devotees now living in South Africa, and the tens of thousands who visit the temples there on a regular basis.

With this book, another piece of Śrīla Prabhupāda’s transcendental pastimes on Earth has delightfully fallen into place. By making this history known to us, Riddha dāsa Adhikārī has done a great service to readers all over the world, who would otherwise not have known about this very important part of our ISKCON history.

As one of the leaders who oversee the preaching presently in South Africa, I am very proud to have a chance to help in all areas of raising consciousness in this very important country at such an interesting time in world history. I am just as proud to be considered a good friend and Godbrother of the author, Riddha dāsa, who has given us this wonderful, enlightening presentation.

**His Holiness Bhakti-tīrtha Swami**

Former Hare Krishna Movement Vice-Chairman

GBC for ISKCON South Africa

Author: *Leadership For An Age Of Higher Consciousness,*

*Spiritual Warrior I, II&III* and *The Beggar I, II&III*

## Preface

During the early part of the nineties, I was entering upon a very important learning and healing stage in my life. I was unaware that I was being prepared for the compilation of all my recollections of Śrīla Prabhupāda’s visit to South Africa.

It was a time when I was deeply involved in meditation and inner self-exploration. Feeling drawn to find out about my past life, I took the opportunity to go back in time. But a funny thing happened: instead of undergoing regression to a previous birth, I travelled back some twenty years to the most important moment in my whole evolution, the time when I was serving Śrīla Prabhupāda.

This overwhelming experience of being in the presence of Prabhupāda was very elevating. It did not matter that I had not gone back into a past lifetime, because now I was with Prabhupāda once again. Warm tears ran down my face while in trance, and for the next few hours I wept continuously, being in constant recollection of His Divine Grace.

Finally, after a period of intense spiritual emotion, I came out of trance, although the state of blissfulness remained with me for many days. That is how it all began. Some months later, while writing down my thoughts, I conceived the idea of writing a book. In this way I experienced Śrīla Prabhupāda’s mercy.

Then, in a devotional spirit, I wrote for more than two years, in order to publish *Destination South Africa* for the Prabhupāda Centennial celebration. It finally came off the press in September 1997, just in time to coincide with one hundred years of Śrīla Prabhupāda’s legacy. Although the book was distributed worldwide and received general approval, I was despondent and felt the need to refine it as much as possible.

By the beginning of 1999, I had decided to present a fuller picture with more precise detail, augmented by further material which had inadvertently been omitted. I also felt the need to explain what happened after His Divine Grace Śrīla Prabhupāda left South Africa.

After further research, I felt I had enough information to give a proper account of those devotees who were fortunate enough to fulfil the wishes of Śrīla Prabhupāda, but whose service activities were still undocumented. With this new information, I then began the process of including the supplementary parts.

In my search for a suitable editor, my Godbrother, Gadāgraj, introduced me to Alan Miles, a semi-retired devotee from Liverpool with a flair for English, who eagerly took on the task. It was as if Kṛṣṇa had sent him, right on cue. That was in June 1999. Since then, we have worked together very closely to produce a fully revised and expanded edition.

I had no idea how long the whole process would take, but now in the year 2002 I feel it is time to make the final offering to His Divine Grace Śrīla Prabhupāda. Indeed, my endeavours would have been fruitless had it not been for the sincere Kṛṣṇa conscious efforts of

all concerned in helping me to produce this book, which will serve as a lasting testimony for the benefit of generations to come.

## Acknowledgements

I am very grateful to my Godbrother, Abhirama dasa, who made available for this special edition the passport Śrīla Prabhupada used in South Africa. Many thanks to Anamika and Sanjaya for their timely help. I am also very grateful to Indupati for his support. In the final push to get this book on the press I am very grateful to Bob Narandas of Narandas Jewellers, Durban for his contribution, and to Champak Lal Soni for his selfless help.

**Riddha Dāsa Adhikārī**

## Introduction

In my research, I came to learn of the great sacrifices that had been made by my spiritual brothers and sisters who helped to bring Kṛṣṇa consciousness to South Africa. I also realised that many Indian residents of South Africa were instrumental in helping to establish ISKCON. This book provides a glimpse of those sacrifices in the early days, without which Kṛṣṇa consciousness would not have flourished in South Africa.

In order to understand the evolution of ISKCON and the *līlā* (pastimes) of its founder, His Divine Grace A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami Prabhupāda, one should first consult the Vedic literature or *śāstra.* One must realise that a bona fide spiritual master is not an ordinary man. Just as an ambassador is an authorised representative of his country, similarly, a spiritual master, who comes in the disciplic succession, *paramparā*, is an empowered representative of God.

Such an empowered spiritual master appears in order to re-establish the eternal principles of religion, *sanātana-dharma*, and to give shelter and knowledge to the conditioned souls so that they may return to their place of origin, the spiritual world.

Lord Kṛṣṇa appeared in this world as Śrī Caitanya Mahāprabhu. In this very rare incarnation He acts as Lord Kṛṣṇa’s devotee. His advent in Māyāpura, India, was at the time of a lunar eclipse on the Phālgunī pūrṇimā evening, 1407 Śakābda, which corresponds to 18th February 1486 in the Western calendar. His appearance was most auspicious. On the evidence of Vedic *śāśtra*, He is revealed not only as God Himself, but also as the most merciful and munificent Personality of Godhead. Being endowed with a beautiful golden complexion, Śrī Caitanya came to show the world how to glorify and render service unto Lord Kṛṣṇa. It was recorded in the *Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam* (11.5.32) five thousand years ago:

kṛṣṇa-varṇaṁ tviṣākṛṣṇaṁ  
sāṅgopāṅgāstra-pārṣadam  
yajñaiḥ saṅkīrtana-prāyair  
yajanti hi su-medhasaḥ

“In the age of Kali, intelligent persons perform congregational chanting to worship the incarnation of Godhead who constantly sings the names of Kṛṣṇa. Although His complexion is not blackish, He is Kṛṣṇa Himself. He is accompanied by His associates, servants, weapons and confidential companions.”

It was also recorded in the sixteenth-century Bengali manuscript *Caitanya-bhāgavata* (*Antya-līlā* 4.126):

pṛthivīte ache yata nagarādi grāma  
sarvatra pracāra haibe mora nāma  
\[*CB Antya-khaṇḍa* 4.126\]

“In every town and village of the world My name will be heard.”

Locana dāsa Ṭhākura, a great *vaiṣṇava* saint, predicted in his book *Caitanya-maṅgala* that a *senā pati*, a “commander-in-chief,” would come and accomplish this mission. Furthermore, in *Nityānanda Sūryodaya*, an article published in a magazine called *Sajjana-toṣaṇī* in the nineteenth century, Bhaktivinoda Ṭhākura predicted that a time would come (not too far in the future) when the chanting of Lord Kṛṣṇa’s name, the *mahā-mantra,* would spread throughout the world. Previous *ācāryas* like Śrīla Bhaktivinoda Ṭhākura also predicted the coming of a great saintly person in the modern age.

Undoubtedly, that person is Śrīla Prabhupāda. It is evident that his life corresponds closely with descriptions given by the previous *ācāryas*. As with most empowered personalities there are special characteristics. Śrīla Prabhupāda’s date of birth, 1st September 1896, was indeed highly auspicious. Born Abhay Charan, an astrologer predicted that by the age of seventy he would travel the world and disseminate religion. His horoscope showed that this exalted, renounced teacher would establish 108 temples around the world.

However, the most striking prediction is the one given in the fifteenth-century text *Bhāgavata Māhātmya*, in which the great sage Nārada Muni is talking to Bhakti (devotion personified), and Bhakti says to him, *idaṁ sthānaṁ parityajya videśaṁ gamyate mayā*: “I will leave this country (India) and go abroad.” The book also states that personified Bhakti would first go to South India and then to Vṛndāvana, before leaving India to travel to the western world.

Śrīla Prabhupāda’s movements seem to parallel this. Furthermore, Śrīla Prabhupāda’s departure from India at that time resembles that of the personality Bhakti.

When Abhay Charan (as Śrīla Prabhupāda was then known) met his spiritual master, His Divine Grace Bhaktisiddhānta Sarasvatī Ṭhākura Prabhupāda, a spiritual revolution began that was set to change the world. The year was 1922, and a hesitant Abhay Charan felt reluctant about seeing what turned out to be one of India’s foremost Vedic scholars and saintly personalities.

At that time, Abhay Charan had already aligned himself with Mohandas Gandhi’s “Quit India” movement, and was of the opinion that religion was not the answer in India’s hour of greatest need. India at that time was part of the British Empire, and before religion could be practised, Abhay would argue, “India must be an independent country.”

However, his friend Naren Mullik was persistent and persuaded Abhay Charan that His Divine Grace Śrīla Bhaktisiddhānta Sarasvatī Ṭhākura Prabhupāda was no ordinary person.

In a private conversation on 17th March 1973, Śrīla Prabhupāda said:

So I went to see His Divine Grace Śrīla Bhaktisiddhānta Sarasvatī Ṭhākura in 1922 in that Uldanga building, and at that time he was sitting on the roof. There was a small house-room and we were welcome, because they thought Naren Mullik was a very rich man and he had contributed some money. So we went to see \[Bhaktisiddhānta Sarasvatī Ṭhākura\] Prabhupāda and offered our obeisances. So immediately he said that “You are all educated men—why don’t you preach Lord Caitanya Mahāprabhu’s message throughout the whole world?” So I replied to him, “Sir, we are a dependent nation; who will hear our message? We can talk these things after we get independence”—because I was politically minded at that time.

Śrīla Bhaktisiddhānta countered Abhay Charan’s protest by saying that Kṛṣṇa consciousness was not dependent upon Indian politics, or any type of politics for that matter; more important was the message of Kṛṣṇa consciousness. Furthermore, it was so important that it could not wait.

Abhay Charan was very impressed by Śrīla Bhaktisiddhānta Sarasvatī Ṭhākura’s urgency and conviction. After listening to his lecture, he realised that sitting before him was a truly saintly and erudite personality. Accepting him as his spiritual master, he took initiation from Śrīla Bhaktisiddhānta Sarasvatī Ṭhākura in 1933, in Allahabad.

Abhay Charan’s initiated name was Abhay Caraṇāravinda. *Abhay* means fearless and *caraṇāravinda* means lotus feet. Abhay Caraṇāravinda took to heart the order of his spiritual master to preach the message of Śrī Caitanya Mahāprabhu throughout the world.

In 1944 he started publishing a fortnightly magazine in English called *Back to Godhead*. In 1947 the Vaiṣṇava Society conferred upon him the title of Bhaktivedanta: one who has understood that the end of all knowledge is to engage in devotional service. He was now known as A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami. It was not until much later that he became known as Śrīla Prabhupāda.

In 1955 he retired from family life and took up full-time devotional service. In September 1959 he entered into the renounced order known as *sannyāsa*. Between 1962 and 1965, with much dedication and devotion, he meticulously translated the First Canto of *Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam* and had it published.

In 1965 he left India and sailed to America. On board, Swamiji, as he was then affectionately known, suffered two heart attacks, but with the help of Lord Śrī Kṛṣṇa he survived. Over the next twelve years, he was to establish more than 108 temples all over the world.

Śrīla Prabhupāda had a worldwide vision for spreading Kṛṣṇa consciousness and South Africa was part of that vision. It is evident that early on Śrīla Prabhupāda conceived the idea of coming to South Africa, and in a letter to an enthusiastic aspirant interested in Eastern philosophy, dated 3rd July 1971, he wrote:

I am so glad to hear that you would like \[to know\] if we could open one branch of our International Society for Krishna Consciousness (ISKCON) there in Cape Town, South Africa. That would be very nice. So if you can arrange for our reception, we may go there immediately, and even I may go on my way back from India and establish one centre. Undoubtedly this movement is very sublime and anyone who takes to it becomes happy.

There are so many spiritual groups; that is all right. But actually their method cannot help us to make real spiritual progress. This Kṛṣṇa consciousness movement is completely different and sublime at the same time, and very easy to take to also. Anyone who takes to it seriously, their life will become perfect.

Śrīla Prabhupāda dearly wanted Kṛṣṇa consciousness to reach the shores of South Africa. His aim in creating the movement was to fulfil his spiritual master’s vision of spreading Śrī Caitanya’s message throughout the planet, inundating everyone with love of Godhead. However, unlike America or the countries of Europe, South Africa was difficult to enter. It would not be easy to start preaching there as the country at the time was under a strict regime of apartheid. This policy focused heavily on the bodily concept of life, separating people according to race and colour.

This is completely at odds with the philosophy of Kṛṣṇa consciousness which propounds that we are not these bodies but spirit souls. Kṛṣṇa consciousness is transcendental to bodily distinctions perceived in the material world. For the rulers of South Africa, such a concept of the soul would have thrown their discriminatory legislature into disarray.

Śrīla Prabhupāda was well aware of this. However, owing to South Africa’s apartheid laws, the Indian government had banned Indian nationals from visiting South Africa and forbidden any kind of trade with that country. In order for Śrīla Prabhupāda to receive permission to visit South Africa, the International Society for Krishna Consciousness first needed to become established there. This book details the events which led to this. In 1965, some 479 years after Śrī Caitanya’s appearance, A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami stepped on board the steamship SS *Jaladutta* in Calcutta and sailed to America. He was almost seventy, and it was the first time he had ever left the shores of India. His ambitious mission was to fulfil Śrī Caitanya’s prophecy.

In the next twelve years, Śrīla Prabhupāda circled the earth fourteen times, touring and lecturing as well as giving spiritual instructions to his disciples. He translated over sixty different spiritual texts into English and in turn had them translated into some fifty different languages. His writings are highly respected by the academic community for their authority, depth and clarity. They are used as standard textbooks in many universities and colleges. Śrīla Prabhupāda founded the Bhaktivedanta Book Trust, which is now by far the world’s largest publisher of books in the field of Indian religion and philosophy.

This book is about the endeavour of those devotees who took up the clarion call of their spiritual master to spread Kṛṣṇa consciousness—specifically in South Africa.

The writing of this book is an attempt to give a true account of the pioneering days of ISKCON South Africa. I pray to tell it as it happened, especially for the devotees who struggled so hard in the beginning and whose only shelter was the order of Śrīla Prabhupāda. South Africa was isolated politically, and in being there one felt completely cut off from the mainstream of civilisation.

I would like to take the opportunity to thank everyone mentioned in this book, both those who have moved on to do other things and those who remained in ISKCON, and also all who had a role to play, however small or vital it may have been. I feel deeply that they gave their hearts over to Śrīla Prabhupāda’s mission. And in this historical account, it is only fitting that they be given due recognition for their service and dedication, and that they may stand as a shining example for future generations.

## Part One

Part One

## The Pioneering Days

Chapter 1  
The Pioneering Days

Even before the first devotees were to arrive in South Africa, the Hare Kṛṣṇa chant had already been heard. George Harrison of the Beatles recorded “Hare Krishna Mantra” on the prestigious Apple Records label in 1969. Disciples of Śrīla Prabhupāda chant the Hare Kṛṣṇa *mantra* on side one and “Prayers to the Spiritual Master” on the flip side:

Hare Kṛṣṇa Hare Kṛṣṇa Kṛṣṇa Kṛṣṇa Hare Hare  
Hare Rāma Hare Rāma Rāma Rāma Hare Hare

nama oṁ viṣṇu pādāya  
kṛṣṇa-preṣṭhāya bhū-tale,  
śrīmate bhaktivedānta  
svāmin iti nāmine

namas te sārasvate deve  
gaura-vāṇī-pracāriṇe  
nirviśeṣa-śūnyavādi-  
pāścātya-deśa-tāriṇe

The record, quickly climbing the charts, became a huge hit and was distributed all over the world. Despite restrictions in South Africa, the record still managed to enter the country. Although difficult to acquire, it was frequently broadcast on the radio and thus reached a wide audience there. Many people were introduced to Kṛṣṇa consciousness through listening to the record “Hare Krishna Mantra,” and some later played a leading part in ISKCON South Africa. One of them became the first initiated European disciple of Śrīla Prabhupāda in South Africa some years later.

While on the World Sankirtan Party in India during December 1970, Bhaktivedanta Swami and his American and European disciples had caused a sensation in the Indian State of Gujarat. One local newspaper, the *Gujarat Samachar*, published an article on the arrival of Śrīla Prabhupāda and his devotees in Surat. It reported how western devotees were seen dancing in procession in the streets, while enthusiastic locals gave them garlands and showered them with flower petals.

When the people of Surat learnt that westerners were taking up the *vaiṣṇava* way of life and giving up those things that were so much a part of European culture, such as illicit sex, meat eating, gambling and intoxication, it caused such a stir that the news spread throughout Gujarat. Some would openly declare, “These foreigners are going to teach us our very own religion.”

The devotees said, “No, we haven’t come to teach you, just to remind you of what you have forgotten.”

Reports of this new phenomenon spread throughout the Indian communities worldwide. Some Hindus were even saying that they had themselves turned from Lord Kṛṣṇa’s teachings, and that these foreigners seemed to have more devotion than they had.

\* \* \*

The fact that Śrīla Prabhupāda desired to visit South Africa was made evident by his comments in a letter in 1970:

My Dear Tamāla,  
Please accept my blessings. I beg to acknowledge receipt of your letter dated 11th May 1970, and noted the contents.  
When you go to London making plans for World *saṅkīrtana*, you may take into consideration the proposal made by Mr Parekh. He said that from Kenya there is invitation for our group to perform *kīrtana* there. So if we go, a group of at least twenty-five, they have already agreed to pay for return journey and accommodation there. So on your way to India you can perform *kīrtana* in some important cities of Europe, and then go to Africa, Kenya. From Kenya you may go to South Africa.

\* \* \*

Long before any ISKCON devotees arrived in South Africa, an Afrikaner, Mr Andre de Wet from Cape Town, had been corresponding enthusiastically with devotees in London in an attempt to obtain Apple Records’ latest release, the *Govinda* album. He also wanted a copy of *Bhagavad-gītā As It Is* and any other books published by Śrīla Prabhupāda. Mr de Wet was in contact with many students and ran a *yoga* drop-in centre for eastern philosophy. He wrote numerous letters in 1971 urging the devotees to come to South Africa. At one point Śrīla Prabhupāda wrote requesting him to arrange facilities for his arrival. Unfortunately, Mr de Wet did not take up this request, although he was later to meet the first of Śrīla Prabhupāda’s disciples that arrived in South Africa in 1972.

\* \* \*

In 1970 a young, aspiring devotee, Kevin Spencer (later Kṣudhi dāsa), from a Los Angeles high school, came in contact with an ISKCON temple. From then on he began the spiritual path, giving up old and bad habits and taking up new ways that were conducive to spiritual development. Once, as Śrīla Prabhupāda was leaving the temple in Los Angeles and getting into a car, the devotees gathered on the pavement offering obeisances to him, and even though there was no room (the entire pavement was taken up by so many devotees) Kevin was also determined to pay his respects. Rather than stand by and look on, in great humility he bowed down beside the gutter. Observing the sincerity of this seventeen-year-old boy, Śrīla Prabhupāda got out of the car and laid his hand on Kevin’s bowed head, blessing him with divine mercy.

Śrīla Prabhupāda was also to give his blessings to Kṣudhi dāsa in a letter sent to several senior disciples on 15th August 1971. It appeared that he was about to bestow all blessings of the disciplic succession. The letter reads as follows: 1

My Dear Mahāṁsa, Kṣudhi, Ṛṣi Kumār, Nanda Kumār, Tuṣṭa Kṛṣṇa, Kṛṣṇa Tulasī, Gurukṛpa, Madhudviṣa Swami, etc.,  
Please accept my blessings. I beg to thank you very much for your kind feelings of appreciation of my humble self and service unto you. You are all helping me in pushing forward this mission of Lord Caitanya Mahāprabhu coming down by disciplic succession to my Guru Mahārāja. So whatever you have spoken, it is simply due to them. I am simply the via medium to receive them on behalf of my Guru Mahārāja, His Divine Grace Bhaktisiddhānta Sarasvatī Goswami Mahārāja Prabhupāda.  
Hoping this will meet you all in good health.  
Your ever well-wisher,  
A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami

\* \* \*

By March 1972, Ṛṣi Kumāra Swami and Kṣudhi dāsa Brahmacārī were starting to make preparations to leave India for South Africa.

It was about this time that I joined the ISKCON temple in Vancouver, Canada. I was 22 years of age. Not long after, I was informed by one of the devotees that a very important and senior disciple of Śrīla Prabhupāda, His Holiness Puṣṭa Kṛṣṇa Swami, was coming to visit the temple. He had recently received *sannyāsa* initiation from Śrīla Prabhupāda in Mexico City. Puṣṭa Kṛṣṇa later recounted:

After leaving Mexico City, I flew with Śrīla Prabhupāda to Puerto Vallarta, where we stayed for a short time while Śrīla Prabhupāda translated and enjoyed the climate and the natural beauty of the environment before flying to Los Angeles. That occasion brought about a meeting with Bahūdaka, the then president of the ISKCON Vancouver temple.

He generously offered me the use of a van, books and the services of several *brahmacārīs* for the travelling *saṅkīrtana* in the summer. When I was in Vancouver I met Roger, who was later to become Riddha. He has a very vivid memory of that meeting. He describes how we spoke then of South African preaching. When I said that one day the two of us might go to South Africa and preach Kṛṣṇa consciousness, I had no idea that Kṛṣṇa would actually arrange such an opportunity for this dream to manifest. Through the agency of Śrīla Prabhupāda that is precisely what did occur.

The temple was buzzing with excitement in anticipation of Puṣṭa Kṛṣṇa Swami’s arrival. Like the other devotees, I was quite eager to meet the new swami. When I saw him, he was literally glowing with the mercy of Śrīla Prabhupāda. Although it was the first time we had seen each other, there was a certain affinity between us as if I had already known him.

He asked me where I was from and I replied, “I was born in Berea, a suburb of Durban, in a place called ‘Krishna Court.’” We laughed at this remarkable coincidence. I also told him about my mother, who had just recently learnt that I had become a Hare Kṛṣṇa monk. Instead of going into hysterics, like many parents of devotees do, she wondered if my birth in Krishna Court had something to do with my taking up Kṛṣṇa consciousness. Calling me by my uninitiated name, Puṣṭa Kṛṣṇa Swami said, “One day, Roger, we will go to South Africa.” It was a remark I took to heart.

I asked Puṣṭa Kṛṣṇa Swami to tell me about his coming to Kṛṣṇa consciousness and he responded:

I joined the Kṛṣṇa consciousness movement in October 1970. I left college as a premedical student in my last quarter and travelled with Gargamuni Mahārāja, the person who brought me to Kṛṣṇa consciousness. We left immediately and assisted the preaching in Dacca, East Pakistan, in March 1971. The Bangladesh war broke out while we were in Dacca, and finally we arrived in Bombay in April 1971.

I met Śrīla Prabhupāda face to face for the first time. At that meeting I offered my *daṇḍavats* to Śrīla Prabhupāda and he was very pleased. He knew that Gargamuni Mahārāja and I had genuinely risked our lives to preach Kṛṣṇa consciousness.

Śrīla Prabhupāda asked me what I liked best about Kṛṣṇa consciousness and I replied, “the *Bhagavad-gītā*.” He said, “That is very good.” After I left the room and Śrīla Prabhupāda was alone with Gargamuni Swami, he referred to me and said, “I will make that boy a *sannyāsī!*” (This was reported to me later on.)

I took *dīkṣā* several weeks later and Śrīla Prabhupāda personally performed the fire sacrifice. He gave me the name “Puṣṭa Kṛṣṇa,” which means “one who gets strength from Kṛṣṇa.”

I had the opportunity to spend many months in Vṛndāvana at a small place given to Śrīla Prabhupāda in Brahmā Ghāṭa. There, Subal Mahārāja and I lived very austerely and relished the locations of Kṛṣṇa’s pastimes and accounts of His associates. We also visited places associated with Mahāprabhu’s *līlā* and the *gosvāmīs* of Vṛndāvana. We often wandered along the banks of the Yamunā River away from the town of Vṛndāvana, chanting the holy names and becoming absorbed in the mood of Vraja.

After leaving India through illness in August 1971, I vividly recall being confined in a hospital in London with jaundice, when my godbrother, Tribhuvanātha prabhu, came to see me out of his kindness and compassion. On my release I received instructions from Śrīla Prabhupāda before returning to the United States. I had been at college before joining and consequently I wanted to preach Kṛṣṇa consciousness at the universities. Śrīla Prabhupāda gave me his blessings.

In the Autumn of 1971 I returned to Gainesville, Florida, where I had been the first devotee to join. I stayed at the temple for several months regaining my strength. The temple was managed by my old friend Amarendra and his wife Gāyatrī.

I then went alone to Tampa, to the University of South Florida, and began preaching. I remained there until Bhāgavata dāsa came with the “Road Show.” It was organised by Kīrtanānanda Swami and Viṣṇujana Swami. The Road Show included 35 devotees. They and Their Lordships, Śrī Śrī Rādhā-Dāmodara, stayed at the Tampa temple for a number of months. The devotees planned and performed their activities throughout the winter of 1971 to 72.

After their departure, I carried on doing *saṅkīrtana* for several months with a handful of sincere devotees. Then I received a call from Hrdayānanda dāsa, who was the then temple president in Houston, Texas. He asked me to come to Houston to take over as temple president there. I agreed, and some of the other devotees went to the Miami temple.

In Houston I too was called by Kṛṣṇa to come forward and take *sannyāsa* from Śrīla Prabhupāda. It was early May 1972, and I had not yet taken second initiation. I simply wanted to preach Kṛṣṇa consciousness and Śrīla Prabhupāda had remembered me with affection. He gave me sacred thread (second) initiation in May 1972, in Los Angeles, and requested that I come to Mexico City in early June to take *sannyāsa*. I did so, being then 22 years old and eager to preach Kṛṣṇa consciousness around the world.

\* \* \*

It was to be almost three years before I would see Puṣṭa Kṛṣṇa Swami again. We met in March 1975 at the Māyāpura festival in India. The Māyāpura festival had just become an annual event, where devotees from all over the world gathered at the birthplace of Lord Caitanya. I had been distributing large numbers of Śrīla Prabhupāda’s books throughout North America on behalf of the Bhaktivedanta Book Trust Airport Sankirtan Party.

Although I was very successful in this during those three years, I still felt that something was not quite right. I was neglecting a certain aspect of my spiritual life and felt a strong need for change. Fortunately, I had the opportunity to accompany the BBT Airport Sankirtan Party when it left New York for India. It was the first time the devotees had gone in such large numbers and I felt that this was the right thing for me to do. I could rejuvenate my spiritual life by visiting the holy places and at the same time have the association of my spiritual master, Śrīla Prabhupāda.

While at the Māyāpura festival I happened to meet Bhāgavata dāsa Brahmacārī, assistant to Brahmānanda Swami, who had been assigned to oversee ISKCON’s development on the African continent. Bhāgavata learned of my success in distributing books in North America and, since I was originally from South Africa, he believed I could be of real service to the South African temple. With this in mind he had contacted Puṣṭa Kṛṣṇa Swami, who was to become the main person in charge of the temple in South Africa.

Bhāgavata dāsa immediately took me to see Puṣṭa Kṛṣṇa Swami, who had an exhibition stand of photographs promoting ISKCON activities in South Africa. The promotion showed that it was a fertile preaching ground. However, he did not want to send just anyone there owing to the sensitive political nature. Although Puṣṭa Kṛṣṇa Swami had been looking for someone to assist him in spreading the movement in South Africa, he had not found a suitable candidate. Because I was born in South Africa, I could return there unhindered. He also knew that my ability to distribute vast amounts of Śrīla Prabhupāda’s books was an asset to the movement. He then kindly offered me the opportunity to accompany him to South Africa.

\* \* \*

This trip could not have come at a better time. Śrī Caitanya had not only given me a new opportunity for service to Śrīla Prabhupāda, but was also fulfilling the words of Puṣṭa Kṛṣṇa Swami, who three years earlier had spoken of my going to South Africa with him. Those prophetic words spoken at our first meeting finally came to fruition. It appeared that Lord Kṛṣṇa had planned this and that I was destined to return to the land of my birth, having left with my parents when I was only a child of four.

Now I had the opportunity to return, not as an embittered person, but as one who was to bring renewed hope and realisation for the future of South Africa. I would be distributing my spiritual master’s books together with the message of love of God.

## The Beginning

Chapter 2  
The Beginning

During January 1972 in Bombay, India, Ṛṣi Kumāra Swami, who had recently accepted *sannyāsa*, asked whether it was a good idea to open a temple in South Africa. Śrīla Prabhupāda replied, “Yes, there is good potential in South Africa.”

It was then decided that Ṛṣi Kumāra Swami and Kṣudhi dāsa Brahmacārī would be the first ISKCON devotees to travel and preach in South Africa to help establish Śrī Caitanya’s movement. Because of certain complications it was another two months before they left for South Africa.

On 1st March 1972—the Appearance Day of Śrī Caitanya—in the holy place, Śrī Dhāma Māyāpura (West Bengal), Śrīla Prabhupāda laid the cornerstone of the Māyāpura building. Kṣudhi clearly remembers:

The day after Gaura Pūrṇimā, 2nd March 1972, I went into Śrīla Prabhupāda’s room, which was just a grass hut. Śrīla Prabhupāda was sitting down with his back towards Lord Caitanya Mahāprabhu’s birthplace. Several of Śrīla Prabhupāda’s Godbrothers and devotees were in the room.

I offered my obeisances to him and said, “Śrīla Prabhupāda, I am going to Africa now. I hope this gives me the opportunity to increase my services for you.”

Śrīla Prabhupāda put his hands behind his head and, while swaying back and forth, said, “Service to Lord Caitanya! Service to Lord Caitanya! Service to Lord Caitanya!”

Now I felt rightly situated in my constitutional position by Śrīla Prabhupāda’s special mercy. I felt fully committed to this mission of bringing Kṛṣṇa consciousness to South Africa. I felt no worries, no fears or anxieties.

For the journey Kṣudhi managed to obtain some money from his mother; it was added to the small sum that he and Ṛṣi Kumāra Swami already possessed. Although they had no formal plan and no contacts in South Africa, the two devotees left India to carry out their spiritual master’s orders. At the time Kṣudhi was suffering from jaundice—an incredibly painful illness. Therefore, instead of going straight to Africa, they went to Switzerland and stayed there until Kṣudhi recovered. A few weeks later he was well enough to travel and they boarded a plane for Kenya.

While in Nairobi they were introduced to some people—mainly Hindus—who were favourable to their cause. One of them, Mr Damji Devji Pattni, well known in Nairobi and South Africa among the Gujaratis, was a very religious man. An admirer of Śrīla Prabhupāda, he was one of the first to bathe his feet. This took place when Śrīla Prabhupāda visited Mr Damji Devji’s home in Nairobi, in September 1971.

That humble gesture, which is an ancient Vedic tradition, is a heartfelt expression of loving devotion by the *bhakta* at the feet of the *sadhu*, the saintly person. This tradition was honoured by Prabhupāda disciples thereafter.

Mr Damji Devji gave the devotees letters of introduction to his friends and family members in South Africa, requesting help and support to establish Kṛṣṇa consciousness there.

The letters of introduction were a step forward, but when Ṛṣi Kumāra Swami and Kṣudhi dāsa first applied for South African visas a difficulty arose. Although the application was sent directly to the Department of the Interior in Pretoria, it was refused. This was a cause of great concern to them—after all, no visa meant no mission. There were no diplomatic relations between Kenya and South Africa at the time, so they were advised to send their applications to the South African visa office in London.

The applications were successful. In addition, devotees of the Rādhā-Kṛṣṇa temple in London sent details of a South African, Mr Andre de Wet from Cape Town, whom they had contacted. Actually, Ṛṣi Kumāra wrote him several letters from Nairobi requesting assistance, but received no reply.

\* \* \*

After waiting five months in Kenya armed with names of several contacts, the devotees felt confident. On 2nd September 1972 they left Nairobi for South Africa.

The two devotees were in western dress to avoid difficulties with the South African authorities on arrival.

South Africa was at that time controlled by a repressive government made up of Afrikaners and English-speaking people. They had colonised South Africa and introduced special laws that did not allow the mixing of races. People were divided into groups according to race. This became known as “apartheid.” Native South Africans, Indians, as well as those of mixed race and many others, were denied basic human rights and suffered great injustice.

Towns such as Johannesburg were segregated into African, European, Asian and Coloured areas. Those of European origin lived in the more opulent and well-kept parts of the city, while those of mixed race and Indian origin lived on the periphery. Those who were of African origin were forced to settle even further afield in deprived areas far from the city. It was as if the darker your skin the further from civilisation you became situated.

At the time, Śrīla Prabhupāda said, 2 “The philosophy of South Africa is simply on the bodily platform. It is all nonsense.”

For the followers of Kṛṣṇa consciousness, people are not judged or labelled by the way they look, but as individual spirit souls. Therefore a person is not categorised according to his or her physical features, but rather by the level of their Kṛṣṇa consciousness.

\* \* \*

The two American devotees sat on the plane not knowing exactly how they were going to be received. With such thoughts and concerns in their minds, they sat quietly praying and chanting on their beads, asking for Lord Kṛṣṇa’s guidance. What happened next was quite remarkable. It was as if Lord Kṛṣṇa sensed their predicament.

Kṣudhi remembers:

I left for South Africa on 2nd September 1972. I had letters from Mr Damji Devji Pattni in Nairobi. We wore western clothes so as not to cause trouble on entering the country. On the flight I looked at several pictures of Lord Kṛṣṇa as I was praying and chanting.

One Indian gentleman saw the pictures and asked, “Where did you get those pictures?”

I told him that I was a devotee of Kṛṣṇa and that I was on my way to South Africa to establish a temple there. “Take a look at these letters and see if you know any of these people,” I said.

He looked at the letters and said, “This one is for my father-in-law.” It was Mr D.P. Desai from Durban. He thumbed through three more and said, “This one is mine – come and see me tomorrow.”

He was Arvind Gokool and he became our first life-member in South Africa. When I met Arvind Gokool on the plane, I knew for certain that Lord Caitanya was arranging to put his servants in my path so that the mission could be fulfilled.

The two devotees had been enduring great anxiety, and now Lord Kṛṣṇa had sent someone along to help. Although several thousand miles from South Africa and thirty thousand feet in the air, they had made contact with someone they felt could help. During the flight, they were also fortunate enough to meet an Indian swami who helped them find a place to stay on their first night in Johannesburg.

The following morning, Kṣudhi and Ṛṣi Kumāra Swami contacted the persons on the list that Mr Damji Devji Pattni had given them, including Mr Arvind Gokool. Another was Mr Chunibhai Patel, a local businessman, who came and picked up the two devotees and took them to his home in Fordsburg, a suburb of Johannesburg populated mainly by Indians, where they stayed for a while in the hope of making further contacts.

However, the task was not so easy and was made more difficult by a rather harrowing incident. Kṣudhi very vividly recalls:

Mr Chunibhai Patel ran a wholesale vegetable business. Every day he would come home and count the money and then take it to the bank.

One afternoon five African men came looking for the money. They came with guns. They ordered Ṛṣi Kumāra and myself to get under the bed. Four men had guns and the fifth had a crowbar.

The one with the crowbar kept on saying, “Let’s shoot them!” and the ones with the guns were saying, “No.” I thought I had a sixty / forty chance. Sixty per cent I would get shot and forty per cent not! I chanted two rounds on my beads very clearly whilst under that bed. I wanted to make them count as if they were my last!

Somehow or other Mrs Patel tricked the thieves. She locked them out of the bedroom and went to the window and started to shout, “*Chora! Chora!*” \[Thief! Thief!\].

Fortunately, the street was very busy and as the thieves tried to escape they were caught. When the thieves had a gun at my head I felt helpless, totally dependent on Kṛṣṇa. Even though it was a very dangerous incident I felt that Kṛṣṇa was protecting me. It made it all worthwhile. Indeed, it was an opportunity to serve Kṛṣṇa and to feel His presence.

\* \* \*

Shortly after this incident, the two devotees decided that if they were to make any headway they must find more fertile ground. Since there was a large number of Indian people in Durban, they made arrangements to go there by getting in touch with another contact, Mr Dhamji Kara. Mr Patel drove Kṣudhi and Ṛṣi Kumāra Swami to Durban, a bustling former British colonial seaside town teeming with shops and skyscrapers. Since South Africa is located half way between America and Asia it had become a vital sea-trade route. The port of Durban on the Indian Ocean plays an important role even today.

At first they stayed in Mr Dhamji Kara’s house in Overport, a suburb of Durban in the residential area designated for Indians. He was a well-known businessman who owned a string of *sarī* stores called Popatlall’s, the main branch of which was in Durban. Unfortunately, his private residence proved to be somewhat unsuitable for the devotees and their preaching activities.

They next took makeshift lodgings in a hallway. It was arranged by Mr D.P. Desai in the Indian area of downtown Durban. Upon hearing that the devotees were having difficulty with accommodation, a friend of Mr Dhamji Kara, Mr Naginbhai Soni, offered to help by referring them to his son, Champak Lal Soni.

Champak, who often helped visiting swamis by introducing them to the community, assisted the devotees in this way.

While staying in the downtown Indian area of Durban, they were introduced to many key personalities in the Gujarati community. These people, such as Mr J.T. Bhoola, Mr Narandas Valabdass and his son, Bob Narandas, were instrumental in helping ISKCON in its infant years. In addition, Mr Damji Kara and Mr Premji the jeweller were amongst the first to come forward to become life-members.

\* \* \*

On 21st October 1972 in India, at the holy site of Śrīla Rupa Goswami’s *samādhi* in Vṛndāvana, Śrīla Prabhupāda was giving a lecture on *The Nectar of Devotion*. In the middle of his lecture he read out this exciting news: 3

Just now I have received one newspaper cutting. This is from \[Durban\] South Africa. So these two young *gosvāmīs*, they are preaching very successfully in South Africa . . . What is the age of Ṛṣi Kumāra?—*A devotee answers:* Twenty.—Yes! His age is only twenty years. Ṛṣi Kumārā Goswami went, and Kṣudhi dāsa Brahmacārī. They have come to South Africa to spread . . . Kṛṣṇa’s message for South Africa. So by the grace of Lord Caitanya, the message of His Lordship is going on nicely all over the world. And (focusing his attention back on the congregation) you are also members of the society. Try to preach this message: *pṛthivīte āche yata nagarādi grāma*, and you will get strength the more you preach. \[*CB Antya-khaṇḍa* 4.126\]

This was happening unbeknown to the devotees in South Africa, who were finding it difficult to get started. They had signed up only a few life-members—yet soon things would change. Champak Soni, a well-known jeweller in the Gujarati community of Durban, came forward and offered his beach cottage for the devotees’ use.

It was located near Tongaat, roughly twenty kilometres from Durban, at La-Mercy Beach. The holiday home was a unique place, fully detached and standing some fifty metres from the beach. Of modern architectural design, it was a very unusual shape, much like an aeroplane. Consequently, it became known as the Aeroplane House. The new residence had several floors and numerous rooms.

Now that the two devotees had a base to work from, they could concentrate on expanding their preaching work. But before they could start they needed to raise funds. They made arrangements to have a large shipment of their spiritual master’s books sent from the Bhaktivedanta Book Trust in Los Angeles. By distributing these books they would be able to raise money and, more importantly, be able to spread the message of Lord Kṛṣṇa. The shipment finally arrived in Durban in November 1972. It contained 600 copies of *Bhagavad-gītā As It Is*, 400 *Kṛṣṇa the Supreme Personality of Godhead* (trilogy), 75 *Teachings of Lord Caitanya*, 100 *The Nectar of Devotion*, 800 *Śrī Iśopaniṣad*, 500 *Beyond Birth and Death*, 500 *Kṛṣṇa Consciousness*, *the Topmost Yoga System* and 2,000 *Back to Godhead* magazines.

\* \* \*

Mr Ashwin Kara, the manager of Popatlal’s *sarī* store, introduced Kṣudhi to his colleagues within the Gujarati community. One of his friends, Mr Mody, had an up-and-coming small business and was a pious follower of Lord Śrī Kṛṣṇa. He became inspired and offered his services to Śrī Caitanya’s mission with a donation of R2,000, which was then equal to US $3,000, a substantial sum of money at the time. Lord Kṛṣṇa has surely blessed Mr Mody for his kindness.

On numerous occasions, Ṛṣi Kumāra Swami and Kṣudhi gave lectures at many Hindu and Gujarati temples in and around Durban. One such place was Bharat Hall. Hundreds gathered to hear the two young Americans speak. Sometimes they held programmes in Hindu homes, although there was always the risk of being arrested by the paranoid secret police for such clandestine meetings. However, despite the threat of being caught, they felt confident of being protected by Kṛṣṇa Himself and of being blessed for the important role they were playing in those early days.

\* \* \*

One day, back at the ashram in La-Mercy Beach, there was an incident involving a local African man by the name of Goomed. He was the caretaker of Mr Champak Soni’s beach cottage where Kṣudhi was staying. At the time, Mr Soni and his family were staying in Durban on business, so Kṣudhi had to deal with the situation himself.

Goomed had gone to the cottage next door to get some matches and was away for a long time. Kṣudhi felt something was wrong and went looking for him. He asked Goomed’s friend, who looked after the cottage, if he had seen him. He said that the police had picked up Goomed for leaving the house without a “native pass,” a document that all indigenous Africans had to carry and produce on demand. Often, African people were harassed if they failed to produce these passes and put into prison without a hearing for long periods.

Feeling concern, Kṣudhi immediately left to look for Goomed and found him not too far away, locked in an overcrowded police-cage on the back of a truck with other prisoners. When Goomed saw Kṣudhi he was overjoyed. Kṣudhi felt that never in his whole life was someone so happy to see him. He paid the police Goomed’s fine and had him released.

\* \* \*

Despite fear of problems with the authorities, Ṛṣi Kumāra Swami and Kṣudhi held regular preaching programmes. They lectured to groups of up to five hundred. These meetings took place in somewhat covert settings since they were contrary to the practice of apartheid.

The apartheid system did not allow certain races to have large gatherings because of the fear of an uprising. It was seen as a serious breach of the law. The devotees at the time were careful not to get politically involved, for theirs was a purely spiritual mission. For this reason they were temporarily able to escape the scrutiny of the secret police. Their only reason for coming to South Africa was to pass on the spiritual message of Lord Kṛṣṇa as presented by Śrīla Prabhupāda.

In December 1972 Kṣudhi’s parents, Mr and Mrs Spencer, visited their son, concerned about his well-being. They were also well-wishers of ISKCON, and brought a large quantity of incense from the temple’s Spiritual Sky factory in Los Angeles to help their son’s fund-raising efforts. With the arrival of the incense, Ṛṣi Kumāra Swami and Kṣudhi set up an incense distribution factory at the cottage in La-Mercy Beach. When Kṣudhi’s parents arrived and saw that the devotees did not have their own vehicle, they purchased a second-hand American Dodge, which gave them more freedom of movement.

One day, while driving through the countryside, they realised they were almost out of petrol and miles away from any sign of civilisation. Fearing the worst, Kṣudhi’s stepfather told them that their only resort was to start chanting on their beads. As soon as they started to chant, a petrol station came into view.

\* \* \*

At Christmas they all took a break. Kṣudhi, together with his mother and stepfather, decided to visit the famous Kruger National Park, which was at least a two-day drive away. On the way to the park they stopped in Johannesburg to renew Kṣudhi’s visa. At the visa office Kṣudhi was unexpectedly detained and taken to see a member of the secret police. The office was on the 11th floor of the Johannesburg police building, notorious as a place where anti-apartheid sympathisers regularly “committed suicide” in suspicious circumstances. The officer advised Kṣudhi to leave the country and, pointing to the window, said meaningfully, “*This is where they disappear from*.”

Going towards the window Kṣudhi said, “Is that so? Why don’t you come over and have a look down below?”

The officer came over and saw two people waving at him from the ground below. He asked Kṣudhi, “Who are these people?”

Kṣudhi said, “Those people happen to be my mother and father.”

At that point the officer had them brought up. Kṣudhi’s mother was actually a lawyer who had personally helped Śrīla Prabhupāda in setting up the Bhaktivedanta Book Trust in Los Angeles. Kṣudhi added that if the officer was thinking of making life difficult for him then he should think twice, for he would inform the US Government and do everything in his power to make life difficult for him and the South African Government. This stopped the officer from making any rash decisions. He simply refused an extension for the visa. However, this would not prevent Kṣudhi from re-entering the country.

Ṛṣi Kumāra Swami and Kṣudhi flew out of South Africa to Lourenco Marques (present-day Maputo), a Portuguese colony in Mozambique, because they knew that on returning they would be able to gain entrance for at least a further four months.

In Lourenco Marques the devotees took the opportunity to preach there. Kṣudhi met an Indian resident by the name of Mr Vinraven Caaku, who helped the devotees arrange programmes and who translated the talks into Gujarati. Another resident would translate into Portuguese for the benefit of the Portuguese-speaking guests.

Soon after their return to South Africa, word got round that Kṣudhi and Ṛṣi Kumāra the white Americans were meeting with non-Whites, and that the secret police were hot on their trail. This was all too much for Ṛṣi Kumāra Swami. Discouraged, he lost heart saying, “Kṛṣṇa consciousness can’t flourish in South Africa. It is a racist country and you can’t change these people.”

Not long after this, Kṣudhi returned to the La-Mercy Beach cottage to find that Ṛṣi Kumāra Swami had gone. Kṣudhi was dumbfounded. He searched all over, but was unable to find him. Instead, he found a note explaining his disappearance. It was related to personal problems.

\* \* \*

Kṣudhi dāsa Brahmacārī was now completely alone with little money, but he pushed on. It was his sincere desire to please Śrīla Prabhupāda by distributing his books unrelentingly. He would collect donations for books and incense. For two or three books he asked twenty-five rands and for a *Back to Godhead* magazine he asked eleven rands. He walked alone, tirelessly, from shop to shop, the length and breadth of Durban. Kṣudhi owed the Bhaktivedanta Book Trust US $10,000 for the shipment of books already received.

To make matters worse, the government authorities began taking a keen interest in Kṣudhi’s activities. Kṣudhi was not sure, but he felt that all of his movements were being monitored, and he knew that at any time he could be asked to leave South Africa. It was a rather despondent Kṣudhi who wrote to Śrīla Prabhupāda in India to inform him about his unnerving predicament and to find out what to do next. In January 1973 Kṣudhi received a letter from Śrīla Prabhupāda, which stated:

Ṛṣi Kumāra Swami was tempted by *māyā* and he fell, but he came to me here in Bombay. I am very much pleased by the responsibility that you have taken to spread Kṛṣṇa consciousness in South Africa at such a young age. Just like our Nārada Muni who was left alone, somehow or another you were also left alone by Kṛṣṇa’s arrangement. I am praying to Kṛṣṇa that you will have the strength and enthusiasm to carry out this mission. Chant your sixteen rounds and follow the four regulative principles. This will keep your consciousness pure and eager to engage in devotional service.

This letter tells us something of Śrīla Prabhupāda’s desire for Kṛṣṇa consciousness in South Africa. The letter was a source of encouragement for Kṣudhi, who decided to continue with his devotional service in South Africa despite the adverse circumstances.

Kṣudhi was quite relieved. He later explained:

Everything became all right when I got my letter from Śrīla Prabhupāda. I felt I was accepted by Śrīla Prabhupāda into Śrī Caitanya’s *saṅkīrtana* movement. And through my work in Śrīla Prabhupāda’s service I experienced a close connection with all other devotees in India and America. I also felt that both this mission and my life would be successful because Śrīla Prabhupāda was pleased with my effort, and he was praying to Kṛṣṇa that I would have the strength and enthusiasm to carry out this mission.

This was just the kind of inspiration that Kṣudhi dāsa needed to get motivated again. Although he was two miles away from his nearest neighbour and about two thousand miles from any devotee, reading Śrīla Prabhupāda’s encouraging letter helped him to overcome his near- overwhelming despair.

As fate would have it, there were two events which helped pay off the Bhaktivedanta Book Trust debt. The first was that the value of the South African rand rose from US $1.25 to US $1.50, and the second was the success of the incense factory, which had been set up at Champak Soni’s beach cottage.

Meanwhile, the whereabouts of some funds belonging to the South African preaching team in the care of Ṛṣi Kumārā soon emerged in Bombay. Śrīla Prabhupāda wrote: 4

My dear Karāndhara,  
Please accept my blessings. Your letter dated December 28, 1972, is in hand and I have noted the contents carefully.  
A few days back Ṛṣi Kumāra has come to see me from South Africa and he has handed over to me the sum of US $700 in cash. This amount was reserved to pay you for the book sales for December month for South Africa, and it was to be deposited in the BBT there with you. Instead I requested him to give it to me here, and I am depositing it in my BBT here in India. So kindly credit this amount to Ṛṣi Kumāra and debit his bill for the amount of US $700.

\* \* \*

Back in Durban Mr Champak Soni’s children, in particular his ten-year-old son Pradeep, helped with the packaging of the incense to maintain a constant income. In Kṣudhi ’s own words:

I can still hear them counting the incense sticks, “three, six, nine, twelve . . .” and then loudly exclaiming, “fifteen!” as we packaged the incense.

Without the help of Mr Soni and his father, Mr Naginbhai Soni, in the early days, the story of ISKCON South Africa could well have been altogether different.

\* \* \*

Unfortunately, the secret police, after raiding the La-Mercy Beach cottage, were still on Kṣudhi’s trail and were near to catching up with him. In order to escape from the secret police, he drove south to towns such as Cape Town and Grahmstown (near Port Elizabeth), which were mainly populated by Europeans. In Grahmstown he stayed in a flat belonging to Ms Kusum Soni, the sister of Mr Champak Soni. Kṣudhi took every opportunity to preach and even managed to conduct programmes at Rhodes University in Grahmstown.

While at the university he met a student named Greg Kastell who was interested in Kṛṣṇa consciousness and who, incidentally, knew Andre de Wet. They decided to go and see him, but in spite of Mr de Wet’s early enthusiasm and interest in Krsna consciousness, which prompted much encouragement from devotees—not to mention a personal letter from Śrīla Prabhupāda—he proved to be largely unresponsive.

Greg Kastell came from a well-to-do family, originally from Belfast, Ireland. His family later moved to South Africa. Greg was very pious and would help Kṣudhi whenever he was able to, despite his other commitments. Kṣudhi felt that Greg was naturally destined to take up devotional life and become a full-time devotee.

\* \* \*

While Kṣudhi continued promoting Kṛṣṇa consciousness, Śrīla Prabhupāda sensed that all was not well, and thought that as time moved on matters were likely to deteriorate for Kṣudhi. Concerned for the welfare of his young disciple, Śrīla Prabhupāda wrote a letter from New York to one of his trusted disciples, Puṣṭa Kṛṣṇa Swami, requesting him to make arrangements to leave for South Africa and take charge of the mission there: 5

My dear Puṣṭa Kṛṣṇa Mahārāja,  
Please accept my blessings. I have been informed of the situation of the South African programme by Brahmānanda Swami, who is here with me now as personal secretary, and I want to send you there. There is one boy there, Kṣudhi dāsa, who is very young boy, so I want you to go there to take charge of the preaching programme there in Durban.  
One Mr Parekh of the Hindu Mandal has written me inviting me to go there, and they will pay all the tickets. . . so I want to go there from London on my return to India and I can stop there. So you go there and make all the arrangements, and inform me accordingly and I can come.  
You can correspond with Kṣudhi dāsa Brahmacārī at the following address: c/o Popatlal Kara, 201 Gray St., Durban, South Africa, for getting the visas. It will be easier for you to go from London.  
I hope this meets you in good health.  
Your ever well-wisher,  
A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami

Śrīla Prabhupāda’s letter clearly conveys his anxiety for Kṣudhi dāsa. He had good reason. Not long after, in late May 1973, while Kṣudhi was returning to Durban after a preaching programme in Grahmstown, he was arrested by the police who had been trailing him. The police had already raided the La-Mercy Beach cottage earlier that month while he was on a trip to Port Elizabeth. They wanted him out of the country straight away. They got his passport, but not his visa—Kṣudhi had managed to hide it. The visa was on a separate paper, and without this document it was difficult for them to deport him.

Since it was a Friday they gave him three days in which to produce the visa or leave. He was not sure whether they would jail him or throw him out of the country. What he was certain of was that he had to get in touch with Puṣṭa Kṛṣṇa Swami, his successor, who was in London, and request him urgently to come to South Africa before being forced to leave the country or, worse, put in prison.

After the police had left, an anxious Kṣudhi quickly went to Mr D.P. Desai’s place. He related the incident with the police and requested to phone his mother in America as well as Puṣṭa Kṛṣṇa Swami in England. Mr Desai was more than willing to help by providing the facilities for international telephone calls. Kṣudhi at last managed to contact Puṣṭa Kṛṣṇa Swami, who was not quite ready to leave for South Africa. At the time he was arranging a collection of funds for himself and his assistant, Janakarāja, who would be accompanying him. Kṣudhi implored the devotees to come immediately, and although he had little money offered to pay for one of the tickets to South Africa the same day.

The very next day Puṣṭa Kṛṣṇa Swami and Janakarāja landed at Johannesburg Airport, from where they took a connecting flight to Durban.

On Saturday and Sunday, Kṣudhi showed the two new arrivals where everything was kept and how everything was managed. He introduced them to some of the people who were helping. He also supplied a list of names and addresses of others who lived in Durban, such as Mr Naginbhai Soni and his son Champak Soni and their families, Mr J.T. Bhoola, Mr Premji, Bob Narandas, Mr Dhamji Kara and his son, Ashwin Kara, and others. They were also given Greg’s name and the car that Kṣudhi’s mother had bought.

\* \* \*

Kṣudhi told Puṣṭa Kṛṣṇa Swami and Janakarāja to go to Cape Town immediately, far away from Durban, to avoid getting caught by the secret police. That Monday, 1st June 1973, Kṣudhi went to the police. They were now aware that Kṣudhi’s visa had expired. They interrogated him and would at times scream at him in Afrikaans. Kṣudhi protested that he had not understood what they were saying to him. They demanded that since he was in South Africa he should be able to speak in Afrikaans.

They then took him before a colonel in the security police who threatened to force Kṣudhi to eat meat, knowing full well that he was a vegetarian.

Kṣudhi replied, “I hold an American passport. America is practically the only country that supports you. If you harm me, I will do everything in my power to change that.”

Kṣudhi realised that this was to be the end of his ordeal. In his mind he thought of Lord Śrī Kṛṣṇa and surrendered to Him. Kṣudhi felt a strong sense of relief and knew intuitively that whatever happened he was under Kṛṣṇa’s special guidance. At the same time he felt joy that Puṣṭa Kṛṣṇa Swami and Janakarāja had arrived safely in South Africa. Despite future difficulties, he felt that the mission of Kṛṣṇa consciousness would now continue in South Africa.

The security officer then changed his tactics and asked Kṣudhi the name of the organisation he was working for.

Kṣudhi did not want to lie, so without giving the name away he replied, “*Gauḍīya vaiṣṇava sampradāya”* and added, “That is all you are getting from me!*”* He wanted to stop the police harassing devotees that were to come after him. The police took eight long hours to decide whether to send him to America or to arrest him.

Fortunately, Kṣudhi had telephoned his parents the previous Friday from Mr D.P. Desai’s place and had them send an airline ticket. Having the ticket helped in the final decision. After making a final call to Pretoria for clearance, the police decided to release Kṣudhi. They stamped his ticket making it valid for 2nd June 1973 only. That was the very next day. The security police warned Kṣudhi that if he was to stay a day over that date the ticket would be invalid and he would certainly be sent to prison.

The next day Puṣṭa Kṛṣṇa Swami drove him to the airport and a very relieved Kṣudhi caught the plane to America. Puṣṭa Kṛṣṇa Swami and his assistant Janakarāja dāsa then proceeded to the La-Mercy Beach cottage to carry on where Kṣudhi had left off.

Kṣudhi had come with very few possessions apart from some letters of introduction and the blessings of his spiritual master. And he left South Africa only after having planted the seeds for future ISKCON missionary activities. Not only had he managed to preach and build up a network of reliable people, but also he was instrumental in making many South Africans aware of Śrīla Prabhupāda’s teachings. He had also started the incense business with the help of Mr Soni’s children at La-Mercy Beach. By selling incense and distributing books, Kṣudhi had single-handedly managed to pay off the debt owed to the Bhaktivedanta Book Trust.

## Destined for South Africa

Chapter 3  
Destined for South Africa

Originally, Puṣṭa Kṛṣṇa Swami had not planned to go to South Africa. He was already well established in France and since becoming fluent in French had made Paris his home base. He had written a letter to Śrīla Prabhupāda informing him of his preaching activities in France and London. Happening to visit Paris while Puṣṭa Kṛṣṇa was away on business, Śrīla Prabhupāda acknowledged his letter by replying: 6

Please accept my blessings. I beg to acknowledge receipt of your letter dated November 1, 1972, and I am very much engladdened to hear that you have arrived to Europe and that you have enthusiastically begun your work there, opening one centre in France. Yes, such opening of temples, holding daily street *saṅkīrtana*, distributing books, preaching in the schools and colleges, this is our standard programme for injecting Kṛṣṇa consciousness in the world. And if we simply stick to this programme we shall be successful, without any doubt.  
Now you are *sannyāsī*—*sannyāsī* means responsible—you must be responsible for the spiritual progress of the devotees, to see that the right standard is being observed in all departments, just as I am doing. Sometimes I go to the bank, sometimes keep accounts, sometimes preach, hold the class, write books, sometimes cook—*sannyāsī* should be expert in all departments, and he should distribute his experience to others, that’s all. So I think you are the right man for assisting me in this way in European continent and Kṛṣṇa has brought you to the right place, so with great enthusiasm go forward. Thank you very much.  
Yes, everyone of you should write something, so if you have completed any small booklets you may send them to me and I shall see them and send to Hayagrīva for possibly printing.  
Hoping this will meet you in good health.  
Your ever well-wisher,  
A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami

Encouraged by Śrīla Prabhupāda’s words, Puṣṭa Kṛṣṇa Swami had applied himself with added confidence in furthering ISKCON’s activities in Europe. Just after receiving this message he received another letter, dated 11th April 1973, in which Śrīla Prabhupāda ordered him to take charge of the South African *yātrā*.

Although Puṣṭa Kṛṣṇa Swami had preaching work in France, Belgium and Switzerland, he understood the urgency behind his spiritual master’s letter, and decided to travel immediately to South Africa. Little did he know that his role in South Africa was to be a vital one. Śrīla Prabhupāda’s sudden change of mind, which at first seemed like a hasty decision, turned out to be timely, exemplifying Śrīla Prabhupāda’s intuitive power to judge and act in a way that brought about the best result.

He understood the difficulties Kṣudhi had undergone in South Africa. Kṣudhi was very young at the time and the only ISKCON representative in the country. Śrīla Prabhupāda, fearing for his well-being, wanted Puṣṭa Kṛṣṇa Swami—who was only twenty-three years old himself—to go to South Africa to take charge of the situation.

\* \* \*

Puṣṭa Kṛṣṇa Swami thought it a good idea to renew his passport with a more traditional-looking western photograph in order to obtain a visa for South Africa. At the time, he was not sure if the South African government officials would permit a western swami—with shaven-head, dressed in a bright orange cloth and carrying the large orange stick *tri-daṇḍa* (which indicates *sannyāsa*, or renunciation)—to enter the country. Accordingly he wore the dress of a respectable businessman with blond wig and sporty cap. Puṣṭa Kṛṣṇa recalled:

I was penniless (having donated all my own funds to the Paris temple), so I began the arduous task of collecting funds to pay my air-fare to South Africa. There was one South African young man who used to visit the Paris temple. I used to gather information about South Africa from him so that I had a better understanding of what to do and how to succeed in the mission. Until that time, my only impression of South Africa was one of white soldiers in short pants with machine-guns shooting native Africans, together with stories seen on the TV news when I was a very small child.

\* \* \*

Puṣṭa Kṛṣṇa Swami had heard how his Godbrother, Brahmānanda Swami, tried to enter South Africa overland from Rhodesia (now Zimbabwe). He turned up at the border in a van emblazoned with Hare Kṛṣṇa logos, wearing the saffron cloth of a *svāmī* and his shaven head marked with *tilaka*.

Brahmānanda Swami explained to the border authorities that he was on a mission to spread the message of Kṛṣṇa consciousness. Unfortunately, they were not at all impressed with his explanation and refused him entry. In fact, he was told never to try to enter South Africa again. They also made a point of registering his name in their records as an undesirable person.

I found out later about the religious intolerance. It may have been that because the devotees were openly chanting in the streets of Salisbury (now Harare) *en route* to South Africa, the Sankirtan Safari Party was turned back.

It is highly likely that their activities were out of line with the intolerant policies of White rule in Rhodesia. They were probably being informed against at the South African frontier prior to their arrival, because these two countries, both of which were racist and fascist, were in collusion with one another.

The groundwork in South Africa could now only be effected by an entirely different approach, one that was commensurate to the extreme political situation. Having learnt what had happened to Brahmānanda Swami, Puṣṭa Kṛṣṇa Swami knew what to avoid. He dressed conventionally before going to obtain a visa and thus avoided drawing the attention of the South African customs officials.

Puṣṭa Kṛṣṇa knew from his study of Vedic literature and his spiritual master’s teachings that sometimes a devotee preacher may have to adopt various stratagems to achieve the desired goals. It was quite in order to adopt western dress so as not to cause a stir with the embassy officials. This is the principle of *yukta-vairāgya* which means that anything can be utilised in the service of God. This principle was highly lauded by Śrīla Bhaktisiddhānta Sarasvatī Ṭhākura, Śrīla Prabhupāda’s spiritual master.

Apart from the concern about one’s outward appearance, there was a more urgent need to justify the principle of *yukta-vairāgya*. Puṣṭa Kṛṣṇa Swami’s Godbrother Kṣudhi was in the precarious situation of either being expelled or worse, detained in one of the notorious prisons run by the apartheid government. Puṣṭa Kṛṣṇa Swami had this to say about the situation:

I did not want to take a chance in going to South Africa and failing because I wanted to relieve Kṣudhi dāsa, whom I knew was being told to leave by the police. Therefore, I had to be one hundred-per-cent certain of successfully taking over the mission.

Over the ensuing two years we struggled together to create a movement in South Africa, hoping against hope. The situation was very delicate. I did not fear for anything except failing Śrīla Prabhupāda. I wanted to succeed in this mission for the satisfaction of my beloved master, Śrīla Prabhupāda. I always felt as if I had a protective bubble around me in the form of Kṛṣṇa’s mercy. If Śrīla Prabhupāda desired this then Kṛṣṇa would be obligated to bring it about because it was the desire of His pure devotee. I was nothing but Śrīla Prabhupāda’s instrument. Śrīla Prabhupāda was always affectionate to me.

*Janakarāja had just received* brāhmaṇa *initiation and showed much promise. Puṣṭa Kṛṣṇa Swami felt certain that Janakarāja dāsa Brahmacārī would be able to cope with the difficulties they were certain to face whilst in South Africa. Having made up his mind to take Janakarāja with him, Puṣṭa Kṛṣṇa Swami now had to collect more funds for his ticket. It was around this time that Puṣṭa Kṛṣṇa Swami received an urgent telephone call from a very anxious Kṣudhi, urging him to take the next flight and relieve him of his post.*

They landed at Johannesburg Airport and immediately caught a connecting flight to Durban where the anxious Kṣudhi was waiting for them. Janakarāja was amazed—he had never seen anyone rush around as fast as Kṣudhi. Janakarāja was not aware of the circumstances and was amused by the speed at which Kṣudhi did everything. He thought it was like a speeded-up film!

Puṣṭa Kṛṣṇa Swami’s arrival in South Africa heralded the second phase of establishing Kṛṣṇa consciousness in South Africa, building on Kṣudhi’s groundwork.

\* \* \*

The new arrivals were aware that they would be monitored since they were living in an area specifically designated for the Indian community. They stayed about a week at Champak Soni’s beach cottage and planned to travel to the south coast. Before travelling south, they went to Durban and bought a supply of materials such as fragrant oils to continue with the incense factory.

About this time, Champak Soni sent a message to his sister who lived in Grahmstown. He informed her that the new American swami and his assistant had arrived. He wanted her to contact one of Kṣudhi’s contacts, called Greg, who was a student at Rhodes University.

Champak’s sister telephoned Greg at the university, urging him, “Come, come! The American swami is coming tonight.”

That evening Greg made his way to her house anticipating their arrival. Greg waited patiently as it turned from seven o’clock to eight o’clock. Still there was no sign of them and so it went on until almost midnight. Finally, an exasperated Greg got ready to leave. As he opened the door there they were! At last they had arrived from Durban after a long drive.

Since they did not want to inconvenience Champak’s sister any further, they immediately left for the university where Greg had his dormitory accommodation. Puṣṭa Kṛṣṇa Swami had high hopes for the university students. That night two weary persons bedded down for the night in Greg’s room.

To Greg’s amazement the swami and his assistant rose at 5 a.m., while the rest of the students were still sound asleep. The austere swami and his assistant took the traditional cold shower, although Greg said he would run the hot water for them if so desired. The swami insisted on having cold showers, and also insisted that Greg did the same. In so doing Greg took his first steps on the *bhakti-yoga* path. This frenetic activity of shivering and running around in wet towels woke up the other students in the dormitory. They demanded to know the reason for this commotion in the early hours of the morning.

\* \* \*

Being a former premedical student, Puṣṭa Kṛṣṇa Swami was very enthusiastic to preach to the students. Within a day or so he was able to get approval to give a series of lectures to the students. This was such a sensation that the halls were filled to capacity. He was an instant hit on the campus. The students had heard nothing like this before.

Unfortunately, as this was a very prestigious campus, the local in-house plain-clothes security began to inquire as to who they were and what they were about. They interviewed Puṣṭa Kṛṣṇa Swami about his activities at the university. The campus security wanted to know if they were propagating communist ideology, for they were under the impression that they were political agitators infiltrating the university—until it became clear that they were harmless monks.

Seeing that the situation was sensitive, Puṣṭa Kṛṣṇa Swami thought it wise to avoid confrontation and decided to head for Cape Town. Greg stayed on to continue his studies but maintained contact with the devotees. Puṣṭa Kṛṣṇa Swami recollected:

I was only 23 years of age and Janakarāja was slightly junior to me. We were trying to maintain a somewhat regulated programme of *sādhana* while still developing preaching programmes in South Africa. At first I believed that the most fertile field for preaching would be amongst the European community, for it was from there that nearly all of the devotees had come to the world preaching mission that Śrīla Prabhupāda had created. After speaking with a number of people, I discovered that Cape Town was thought to be the “most liberal” of all cities in South Africa, and so I decided we should go there. We took some books and some incense (that Janakarāja and I had made) to sell and help sustain ourselves.

We set off for Cape Town, a very beautiful and cosmopolitan place. We were determined at least to try and preach to the European youth to ascertain if this was a ripe preaching field. I found out that the South African white youth were not particularly frustrated by things in general. They were the beneficiaries of a society that oppressed the other classes.

\* \* \*

Arriving in Cape Town, they rented a storeroom for the incense and books at Weiner Building, 150 Long Street. They managed to survive by staying in hippie communes where they could make inroads. After a short time this proved impractical, so they tried to contact the local students. In Puṣṭa Kṛṣṇa Swami’s own words:

I recall going to Cape Town University with Janakarāja to perform *saṅkīrtana* one day. We sat on the lawn and chanted the holy names of the *mahā-mantra*. Afterwards, while I was giving a short talk on Kṛṣṇa consciousness one of the students threw a cherry bomb (a kind of large exploding firework) at me. I remember it blew up not more than three feet in front of me.

I was quite unshaken by this because I was transcendentally absorbed in the holy names just moments before. Up to that time the students were just laughing and taunting us. However, after seeing how completely undisturbed I was by the laughter and sudden explosion, the students became sober, and some even listened to our presentation.
