We never returned to Cape Town University, but we did become more focused on *saṅkīrtana* in the streets of Cape Town.

Puṣṭa Kṛṣṇa and Janakarāja would simply take a *mṛdaṅga* and *karatālas* and go into the centre of Cape Town, chant the holy names and distribute books and *Back to Godhead* magazines. This was very much Śrīla Prabhupāda’s mood. They found that the so-called “Coloured” population and African people showed the most interest. Puṣṭa Kṛṣṇa would boldly declare:

This philosophy teaches that we are all spirit souls. We are not these bodies. As such, no one is Black, White, Coloured or Indian. These are all designations for the temporary body.

The oppressed people under the apartheid regime were elated to hear this. They appreciated that it was coming from white preachers who were clearly not interested in establishing another racially oppressive religion.

Feeling unsettled, they decided to secure some inexpensive accommodation in a house that belonged to an Afrikaner hippie. A total mess, the walls were painted black and there was junk everywhere. Had it not been for the low rent, they would have found accommodation elsewhere. They redecorated the whole house, converting one of the rooms into a temple-room.

It came about that Puṣṭa Kṛṣṇa Swami’s tourist visa was running out, American nationals not being granted very long “temporary residence permits.” Consequently he often travelled to neighbouring countries or overseas, and this meant that Janakarāja was left on his own. One day the hippie landlord walked into the room and told Janakarāja that he and his friends were going to take an LSD trip that evening. (LSD is an extremely dangerous hallucinogenic drug.) He asked if he would mind.

There was very little Janakarāja could do so he said, “It’s your choice.” As the evening wore on, Janakarāja could feel the atmosphere getting more and more uncomfortable.

In the middle of the night the door suddenly burst open. The hippie stormed into the room in a completely drugged state. His eyes were bloodshot and his six-foot-four figure towered over Janakarāja, out of control. He demanded in an angry tone. “Either you pay more rent or you get out of here! *”* Janakarāja opted for the latter, and in less time than it takes to count to ten he had all the luggage and most of the furniture packed into the blue Colt car, and was gone as if the hounds of hell were after him. Janakarāja, being a newly initiated *brāhmaṇa*, was no longer going to put up with long-haired, drug-crazed hippies.

Janakarāja had packed everything bar the kitchen sink. The passenger side was taken up by a cupboard and there was just about room for himself. He drove the car to Weiner Building at 150 Long Street, and managed to fit all his belongings into the office storeroom. There was just room to lie down and sleep. Since it was an office block, nobody was supposed to live there, so he blacked out the windows to avoid prying eyes.

\* \* \*

Between September and December of 1973, Janakarāja and Puṣṭa Kṛṣṇa Swami were in contact with Greg. Sometimes Greg would come down at weekends and holiday times to join in. Life was so austere. They had no shower facilities. In the early morning they had to stand on the fire escape, which was several floors up, throwing cups of cold water over themselves from a bucket.

Janakarāja had to keep things going on his own with the occasional help of Bhakta Greg. He was not to distribute any books or incense when alone because he would be risking his stay as a visitor. Every three months or so Puṣṭa Kṛṣṇa Swami was forced to leave the country for a week or more and then re-enter South Africa, because he only had a multiple-entry visitor’s visa. There was a constant threat of being asked to leave the country. As Janakarāja was the only initiated devotee there and a relatively new one, Puṣṭa Kṛṣṇa Swami was quite concerned for his safety. When Janakarāja was alone it proved to be quite difficult. It got to such a state of affairs that he would give morning class to himself. At one point he even doubted his own sanity. If it had not been for his Kṛṣṇa consciousness, he might well have suffered.

He remembered well: “The room was very austere. Because I was on my own I wanted to ensure that I ate properly. Since I did not want to cook every day and I was doing a lot of running around, I remember cooking vegetables and re-heating them three days in a row. I would get quite ill on the third day.”

Fortunately, they had accumulated some funds from the book and incense distribution. He decided to move out of the storeroom and into a bed and breakfast accommodation to regain his health and well-being.

After about a week, fortunately for Janakarāja, Puṣṭa Kṛṣṇa Swami returned. He decided that bed and breakfast accommodation was quite unsuitable for a preaching centre. For this reason they rented a house in the European area of Cape Town: 74A Upper Waterkant Street, and called it Śrī Śrī Rādhā-Kṛṣṇa Temple. They printed cards and invited people of every race to join them for the programmes. They also did some preaching amongst the Indian community of Cape Town, but mostly they concentrated on the street *saṅkīrtana* and book distribution. In fact, the vast majority of people that took their books were from the non-White population.

\* \* \*

Puṣṭa Kṛṣṇa remembers the encouraging response from the African people:

Our first encounters with the African community came in Cape Town. Factually, those people appreciated the simplicity of the philosophy and the non-sectarian message, and also the fact that we were not trying to supersede Christianity, but were actually fulfilling the goal of religious philosophy. We didn’t go so much to the African townships at the time, mainly because of the apartheid, but I didn’t withhold Kṛṣṇa consciousness from those people we encountered. This was my own feeling. We already had the experience of Kṣudhi dāsa being ejected because he represented something that wasn’t understood by the South African authorities, and rather than jeopardise our early years we steered away from areas of sensitive activity.

My main mission in going to South Africa was to try to establish Kṛṣṇa consciousness and bring Śrīla Prabhupāda there to preach and preserve Kṛṣṇa consciousness for future generations. If I had been a blatant challenge or threat to the South African policy of apartheid, then I would also have been ejected and our society banned. I had the benefit of learning from the experience of Kṣudhi and from Brahmānanda Swami, who were both banned from South Africa.

From this account it was clear that Puṣṭa Kṛṣṇa’s level-headed approach kept the Kṛṣṇa consciousness movement in good standing, but he always presented the truth, preaching and distributing his spiritual master’s books vigorously without discrimination. Mass conversion was never a policy, but rather, profuse book distribution.

\* \* \*

Puṣṭa Kṛṣṇa Swami proved himself to be quite a handyman. He applied his carpentry skills in building an altar and a *vyāsāsana* (seat) for the spiritual master. It was quite a remarkable thing to see a *sannyāsī* working around the house doing alterations and repairs. Once it was completed and made presentable, they started arranging programmes and inviting guests.

In Cape Town there were many pious Malay people who were friendly to the devotees and appreciated their efforts. They were not particularly well off, since their main source of income was selling flowers in the downtown area. With a nice smile they would always donate flowers to the temple. Their offerings were made with humility as an act of devotion. Puṣṭa Kṛṣṇa Swami, Janakarāja and Greg would regularly go into town and chant the Hare Kṛṣṇa *mahā-mantra* and distribute Śrīla Prabhupāda’s books.

The Sunday Love Feast was becoming more popular and mostly attracted young European hippies. It was at this time that the devotees had their first real help. It came from a young Tamil woman, Saras Pillay; Saras was a teacher by profession. After teaching she would join the devotees in devotional service. Before going to work, she would also cook breakfast for the devotees, but due to the apartheid laws she was unable to stay at the temple-house. The house was in a European area, and since Saras was Indian they made out she was a servant and so she stayed in the servants’ quarters. Saras had a pleasant personality and endured this.

\* \* \*

The devotees were encouraged by the book distribution, which was going very well. When Śrīla Prabhupāda learnt of this he wrote a letter from Vṛndāvana to Puṣṭa Kṛṣṇa, who was in England renewing his visa and trying to recruit more devotees. Śrīla Prabhupāda wrote: 7

Your report is encouraging to me, that you distributed 110 *Gītās* in two days in Cape Town, South Africa. You also find that there is good preaching to be done there amongst the intelligent Indians and Europeans. And for the people in general, you can hold *kīrtana* and distribute *prasādam* sumptuously. So I understand that you are now at Bhaktivedanta Manor assisting Mukunda in strictly following the *paramparā* and encouraging all the devotees in that direction. When your work is done there, you may return to South Africa. And I have asked Mukunda to help you with some men for South Africa. I am also very pleased with the reports I am getting from Brahmānanda Swami, so please co-operate with him in Africa.

At the same time, Śrīla Prabhupāda wrote another letter to Mukunda dāsa (later Mukunda Goswami) with regard to Puṣṭa Kṛṣṇa Swami’s arrival at Bhaktivedanta Manor in England, in which he said: 8

That Puṣṭa Kṛṣṇa Mahārāja is assisting you in raising the standard at Bhaktivedanta Manor is also good news. He has written me that he is needing English boys to join him in South Africa, as the English of all nations have free entry there. He is very hopeful of preaching and distributing books in Cape Town. So if possible, help him by offering some English boys, and after some time he may go to South Africa with the boys.

Janakarāja and Saras Pillay would often have long talks about Kṛṣṇa consciousness. It seemed to Janakarāja that Kṛṣṇa consciousness could actually flourish.

One day there was a knock at the door. Janakarāja opened it. Before him stood a six-foot man wearing shorts and a safari jacket; on his head was an odd looking hat. Reflecting that some pretty strange people arrive at Kṛṣṇa’s door, Janakarāja greeted him, saying, “Hare Kṛṣṇa! Have you been here before?”

The visitor replied in a resounding voice, “*Haribol!* I haven’t seen a devotee in over a month!” It was Jagat Guru dāsa Brahmacārī, an American devotee sent from Kenya by Brahmānanda Swami to assist Puṣṭa Kṛṣṇa Swami with the South African *yātrā*. He moved in, brought over his belongings and changed into devotional clothing.

Unfortunately, that very day, Jagat Guru decided that it was not *vaiṣṇava* etiquette to have a woman in the temple where celibate monks reside. Janakarāja had the unpleasant task of breaking the news to Saras Pillay. Since he regarded Jagat Guru as his elder, there was not much he could do about it. She moved out, but continued to visit.

Now there were only two of them. Janakarāja saw Jagat Guru as his mentor, since he was older. However, when it came to assigning who did what around the temple, Jagat Guru insisted that Janakarāja should be the temple president, while Janakarāja claimed that Jagat Guru should be. Being the only ones there, the situation became quite awkward and the misunderstanding went on for some time. Eventually they decided that it would be more prudent to get away for a time. Both were eager to go and preach to the outside world, so they filled the car with necessities for travelling and were soon on the road. This gave them a sense of purpose and direction.

\* \* \*

Once, they were driving through the South African bush taking turns to drive. The countryside was uninhabited. Jagat Guru was going through the rough terrain at quite some speed and enjoying himself dodging bushes, rocks, animals, etc. When he got tired he handed the wheel over to Janakarāja, who thought that if Jagat Guru could drive as he saw fit then he too could go darting through the bush.

Jagat Guru, however, objected to him speeding and asked him to slow down, but Janakarāja ignored his request. This incensed Jagat Guru, who insisted that he slow down, but Janakarāja’s response was to drive all the faster! This was too much for Jagat Guru, who demanded that he pull over and step out of the car with him.

When Janakarāja brought the car to a screeching halt, his irate partner leapt out, but he declined to follow. Jagat Guru, six feet tall, could have passed for an American football player. Janakarāja, knowing him to be his senior in some respects, judged it wiser to let him cool off and sped off into the bush.

After driving for a few minutes, he turned back to pick up Jagat Guru. They resolved their differences and drove on more carefully.

\* \* \*

Between April and May of 1974, Puṣṭa Kṛṣṇa Swami once again returned to South Africa. This was just as well for Janakarāja because his temporary residence permit had expired. On the orders of Puṣṭa Kṛṣṇa Swami, he left for Rhodesia. He was told to buy a one-way air ticket for himself.

On arrival at Salisbury Airport, he was refused entry because he did not have the required money and had no return ticket. Not knowing what to do with him, the immigration put him up for the night in a secure airport hotel. He was under guard. The next morning they gave him the option to go anywhere on the next flight. Janakarāja wanted to return to South Africa, but knowing that this was impossible—because without a return ticket he would not be permitted to enter—he opted for Zambia. On arrival there he was again refused entry by the immigration officer for the same reason.

Janakarāja was left with nowhere else to go. He could neither enter the country nor leave it. He was left standing on the tarmac in a kind of no-man’s-land. The immigration officer simply ignored him and saw to the other passengers.

It was then that Janakarāja spotted a gentle-looking Gujarati businessman, who fortunately understood the situation and decided then and there to support Janakarāja’s mission by enrolling as a life-member. This enabled him to buy an air ticket on the spot, and with the onward ticket he was allowed to enter Zambia.

\* \* \*

Janakarāja stayed at the local temple in Lusaka for several weeks, where by coincidence he met Puṣṭa Kṛṣṇa Swami who was on an overland preaching tour. Puṣṭa Kṛṣṇa Swami made arrangements for Janakarāja to return to England and recruit likely candidates for the South African *yātrā*.

Once in England, Janakarāja joined the book distribution party at Bhaktivedanta Manor and distributed books in London. There he teamed up with Pārtha-sārathi dāsa Brahmacārī and realised at once that he would be a good devotee for the South African *yātrā.* Janakarāja asked him whether he would like to join the team of South African devotees. Pārtha-sārathi was not happy in his service at the time. Instead of distributing books, they were distributing devotional records. Thinking that if he were in South Africa he would be able to do books again, he agreed to go.

Pārtha-sārathi was a well-educated Englishman and a sincere devotee. The temple president, Prabhaviṣṇu dāsa, did not want to loose one of his best devotees and refused him permission to go. When Puṣṭa Kṛṣṇa Swami learnt that Prabhaviṣṇu was not going to let him go, he spoke to Śrīla Prabhupāda about taking Pārtha-sārathi to South Africa. Śrīla Prabhupāda thought this to be a good idea and the matter was resolved soon after. Janakarāja’s intuition had paid off. In due course, Pārtha-sārathi proved himself to be an extremely valuable member of ISKCON South Africa.

Pārtha-sārathi had no idea what South Africa was like, except for what he had seen and read in Tarzan comics and books as a child. He thought it would be nothing but jungle, so when they arrived in urban Johannesburg during the winter of 1974 he was surprised, to say the least. He was looking forward to some hot weather and was hoping maybe to see Tarzan swinging from the trees! Johannesburg was like any other western city, with few trees and certainly no Tarzan. That night they took an overnight train to Cape Town to join Puṣṭa Kṛṣṇa Swami.

\* \* \*

At the temple, Puṣṭa Kṛṣṇa Swami tried to hold regular programmes in the mornings and evenings, but it proved to be difficult for them to maintain the building and find time for preaching because they were constantly repairing and cleaning it. Brahmānanda Swami, who was the overall GBC for the continent of Africa, informed Śrīla Prabhupāda of the difficulty in Cape Town and suggested that it would be easier for the devotees in South Africa to do travelling *saṅkīrtana*. Śrīla Prabhupāda agreed and wrote to Puṣṭa Kṛṣṇa Swami: 9

Yes, I think that the advice given by Brahmānanda Mahārāja is sound, namely not to worry about maintaining a permanent centre in South Africa, but travelling with *saṅkīrtana* there for distributing books and preaching, which you say is a good field there. We do not have many men; it requires at least five men to maintain a centre, so I approve of your plan to close the centre and travel in South Africa.

Puṣṭa Kṛṣṇa Swami, having received approval from his spiritual master, took the decision to relinquish the house and travel to Durban. When leaving Cape Town, a young European hippie joined the travelling party, but was accompanied by a girlfriend who did not want him to go. They did travel with the devotees for a few days, but then left to return to Cape Town. One more devotee from England, Dhīrāṅga dāsa Brahmacārī, arrived and joined the small band. His help was much needed during this difficult time.

\* \* \*

For one thing, there was nothing comparable to the Vietnam War to stir up South Africa’s youth on questions relating to the purpose of life. As well as trying to convince young South African Whites of the validity of an ancient Vedic tradition, they had to confront a rigid South African family structure. Most Europeans were not materially exhausted or fed up, but led comfortable lives at the expense of other races, not always out of malice, but owing to *karma* and the legalised racism of the day.

Added to these difficulties, the apartheid system not only distanced races, but also actively discouraged integration. Greg Kastell recollects how the army draft was another obstacle for young people:

Because I had gone to live in Rhodesia, I missed the draft—which was fortunate. I left South Africa during high school and returned later. All South African Whites would have to attend the army camp at the age of eighteen. This was mandatory and the army attendance would go on for many years after. Somehow I missed it.

Puṣṭa Kṛṣṇa Swami was always concerned with how to encourage white South Africans to become devotees. Lord Śrī Kṛṣṇa had a plan and that was still to unfold. If only one or two university students could use their intelligence to push the movement forward, this would be a blessing. But there was never a conversion policy to make full-time devotees, and enrolment was always voluntary.

Puṣṭa Kṛṣṇa Swami stresses the importance for South African devotees:

In any case, we were presenting Kṛṣṇa consciousness according to *paramparā*, or disciplic succession, without deviation. Everything was descending through the mercy of Śrīla Prabhupāda and Kṛṣṇa. I was always anxious to see some sincere South Africans come forward and take their place in Kṛṣṇa consciousness, since I knew that some day I would probably be forced to leave the country in the manner that Kṣudhi dāsa was. I thought, “How will we establish our mission in South Africa without a few dedicated South African devotees?” I was, of course, very pleased and duty-bound to preach Kṛṣṇa consciousness to large crowds, to distribute books on *saṅkīrtana*, and to make new life-members who gave us the basics for expanding and continuing our activities, but in my heart and mind I was praying and waiting for those souls to come forward.

*En route* to Durban from Cape Town, they stopped over in Grahmstown and decided to go and see Bhakta Greg. Puṣṭa Kṛṣṇa Swami spoke to Greg seriously about devotional life and tried his best to convince him of its benefits:

Up to this point, it was a real tug-of-war for the soul of Greg Kastell. He had a lot of things going for him: he had a girlfriend; he was doing well at university; and he came from a well-to-do family. Most probably he had performed some pious deeds and even may have performed some Kṛṣṇa conscious activities in previous lives. Because he was searching for spiritual life, it was not really difficult to show him the path of Kṛṣṇa consciousness.

Greg took the opportunity to join the devotees on their travelling party and to take up spiritual life permanently. At this point he dropped out of university. Before leaving the campus, Jagat Guru shaved Greg’s head leaving only a *śikha*, a tuft of hair, as a sign of dedication to Lord Kṛṣṇa.

Puṣṭa Kṛṣṇa explains that things were to change.

When Greg finally came to join us, he told us that he had never had to do the laundry or clean anything in his entire life. These were duties performed by the African maids.

Part of the humility of devotional life is to wash one’s own clothes. He also did the Swami’s laundry and many other menial tasks during his *brahmacārya* or student life.

The travelling *saṅkīrtana* party now consisted of Puṣṭa Kṛṣṇa Swami, Pārtha-sārathi, Janakarāja, Jagat Guru and Greg Kastell, now a *bhakta* and the first European South African to join. They all managed to squeeze into Kṣudhi’s old Dodge, which they were still using, and somehow or other got to Durban before the car finally broke down. The car had to be written off and was sold for scrap. It raised a hundred rands.

On their first night in Durban they took rest on the beach, only to be moved on by the police. Pārtha-sārathi finally found a place on the north beach-side near Umhlanga Rocks, where they bedded down in sleeping bags on a grass verge. The very next day, Puṣṭa Kṛṣṇa Swami met Mr Champak Soni in Durban and got the keys for the beach cottage at La-Mercy Beach, some twenty kilometres outside Durban. This again became a base for the travel-worn devotees.

The devotees set up a temple-room for the Gaura- Nitāi Deities, which Puṣṭa Kṛṣṇa Swami had brought back from Navadvīpa, West Bengal, the birthplace of Śrī Caitanya Mahāprabhu.

Being in a remote place meant it was difficult without transport, but then one of Bhakta Greg’s friends, Roma Kearney, turned up while on holiday. A student at Rhodes University, she had come to look up her old college friend, Greg. She arrived in a yellow Volkswagen Beetle, which she later allowed the devotees to use. It became invaluable to them and their preaching.

Roma was hoping to major in psychology. Like Greg she was also a natural seeker of the truth and wanted to know what Kṛṣṇa consciousness was about. She was very much attracted to the philosophy, and particularly to the delicious small bread sticks that Puṣṭa Kṛṣṇa Swami used to cook for *prasādam*! When she too opted out of university and her short visit turned into a permanent one, she was given a room and a bathroom for her own use. The devotees knew that this was in line with Śrīla Prabhupāda’s wish to have both men and women in the temple. Roma became the first white South African woman to join, and had a role to play in the development of ISKCON South Africa.

La-Mercy Beach, being in a remote area, gave some of the *brahmacārīs* like Pārtha-sārathi the opportunity of going door-to-door in the nearby town of Tongaat. He met with much success distributing Śrīla Prabhupāda’s books to the Hindu community. His endeavours were like planting seeds in a garden, which were to bear fruit later.

\* \* \*

In June, Brahmānanda Swami, the GBC for Africa, received a letter from Śrīla Prabhupāda about his eagerness to come to South Africa:10

I was glad to receive your second letter and the letter from the Ministry of Home Affairs stating that they have no objection to my coming to Kenya. Yes, I would like to go there after the Vṛndāvana ceremonies. Hopefully, you will have joined me by then and we can go together to Kenya, and from there to South Africa where Puṣṭa Kṛṣṇa Mahārāja is trying to arrange a programme for our coming.

Meanwhile, Puṣṭa Kṛṣṇa Swami was developing relationships with the Hindu community in Durban. Mr Ashwin Kara, the Manager of Popatlal Kara at the time, describes as follows:

Even if arrested for mixing with white devotees, we were not bothered because we were serving Śrī Kṛṣṇa. We had heard about the Hare Krishna movement from overseas, but now was our chance to participate. I would take Puṣṭa Kṛṣṇa Swami and arrange meetings with twenty-five top Hindu businessmen, mostly from the Gujarati community. We also watched a movie called *The World of Hare Krishna.* As a result, many of those people became life-members and received sets of books and membership facilities from Puṣṭa Kṛṣṇa Swami.

Lacking adequate transport for the increasing number of devotees, Puṣṭa Kṛṣṇa Swami decided to purchase a vehicle, a brand-new Volkswagen Combi van, at a cost of 3,000 rands. The devotees had painstakingly collected the money for this van. Once they had bought the van, almost all their savings were gone, but Puṣṭa Kṛṣṇa Swami was expert in handling money matters and this proved to be the best investment to date. This van was destined to be seen all over southern Africa. Puṣṭa Kṛṣṇa Swami and the devotees drove it to Rhodesia, Zambia and Malawi. The van served for practically everything they had to do.

Sometimes they would put speakers on the roof-rack to announce their programmes and chant in public places. Now that they had a serviceable vehicle, they were able to operate in territories further afield.

\* \* \*

Eventually La-Mercy Beach proved unsuitable for preaching purposes, mainly because of its remote location.

One day, while filling up at a petrol station, they were approached by the security police and warned: “We know what you are up to. We are monitoring all your activities.” The devotees thought it best to leave La-Mercy. In late 1974 they made preparations to move to Pretoria and surrounding areas in the Transvaal. It was about this time that a very encouraging letter came to Puṣṭa Kṛṣṇa Swami from Śrīla Prabhupāda, in which he stated that Greg and Roma had been accepted for initiation: 11

Please accept my blessings. I am in due receipt of your letter dated 24th September 1974 and am glad to read the contents. Your success with the South Africans there is very good. I have initiated the boy and girl, and their spiritual names are as follows: Gokulendra dāsa and Rocanī devī dāsī. You can chant on their beads.

On their way to Johannesburg, the devotees decided to stop at towns which had large Hindu populations. Their first stop was at a town called Greytown. After that they passed through Estcourt, Ladysmith, Dundee and Newcastle, where there were substantial Hindu communities.

Jagat Guru initiated a unique system of enrolling life-members, which he had tried and tested in Kenya. It was simple but effective. In each location where the devotees stopped they would book the local community hall for one night. They would advertise, and local Hindu leaders would have leaflets printed in advance in Gujarati and English, inviting all Hindus to meet the American *svāmī* and European devotees. An added advantage was that there was, practically speaking, no television at the time in South Africa. It was very successful everywhere, with up to one thousand people attending on some nights.

On the stage Puṣṭa Kṛṣṇa Swami would set up an altar for the Gaura-Nitāi Deities, while the devotees put up a stand for the books in the hall. At the beginning of each programme, the devotees would conduct a big *kīrtana* and soon have the audience clapping and singing along. Pārtha-sārathi was in charge of the sound and lighting equipment and would show the film called *The World of Hare Krishna.* Puṣṭa Kṛṣṇa Swami would then give a talk on the Kṛṣṇa conscious philosophy, and at the end request people to put up their hands to become life-members. They would then be presented with sets of Śrīla Prabhupāda’s books, which were collected from the edge of the stage.

Pārtha-sārathi recalls:

We went from Durban to Pretoria and decided to stop at each town with an Indian community to put on a programme and make life-members on the way. This was a sensational success. So a team of us, Puṣṭa Kṛṣṇa Swami, Jagat Guru, Janakarāja and Gokulendra, travelled in the yellow van, while Rocanī drove along in her Beetle.

The first stop was Estcourt. We stayed three or four days there. Each night we stayed we would hold a big programme in the local community hall, attracting over a thousand people. I was permanently responsible for setting up the 16mm projector, the screen, and the microphones for the speakers. We would show *The World of Hare Krishna,* our first documentary film, and Puṣṭa Kṛṣṇa Swami generally saw to it that the Deities were set up.

The programme would be straightforward—*kirtana*, a film and a talk. At the end Puṣṭa Kṛṣṇa Swami would say, “Who in the community would like to support us and become life-members?” It could be very powerful. And then a five minute explanation of what life-membership meant: “You receive a life-membership card and are able to stay at our Kṛṣṇa consciousness temples; you also receive a whole set of Śrīla Prabhupāda’s books.”

A devotee would then hold up the books and Puṣṭa Kṛṣṇa Swami would continue, “We will send you *Back to Godhead* magazines periodically. Who in the community would like to become a life-member?”

Usually a prominent man would put up his hand and walk up to the stage and collect his books. While being presented with the books, we would all clap. Then Pusta Krsna would add: “Now, who else would like to become a life-member?”

And in this way we would make five to seven, or even ten, life-members in one night. It was great fun to see a whole set of Śrīla Prabhupāda’s books going out. In the daytime Puṣṭa Kṛṣṇa Swami would go round to collect their cheques and completed application forms. We did this in every town right up to the Transvaal. This process was very potent. Besides creating a secure financial basis, we were giving out large numbers of Śrīla Prabhupāda’s books.

Owing to the overwhelming success, they decided to hold similar programmes throughout South Africa. With this in mind, they staged further programmes in Durban and Pietermaritzburg at later dates.

The large Hindu community showed a lot of enthusiasm for the presentation of Kṛṣṇa consciousness by western devotees, who reminded them of their own culture. Because of the oppressive nature of the apartheid system, the Hindus were not able to fully express their religious sentiments. The devotees’ presence not only was a blessing in an oppressed country, but also revitalised and strengthened the faith of the Indian people.

Not all of the enthusiastic Hindus were able to pay the membership fees in one go, so Puṣṭa Kṛṣṇa permitted the use of post-dated cheques. In this way the people got the books and the facilities.

On one occasion, the travelling *saṅkīrtana* party ventured through Northern Transvaal to Louis Trichardt, near the Rhodesian border, where there was a sizeable Indian community. During a programme in a local community hall, the devotees proceeded as usual. Puṣṭa Kṛṣṇa Swami distinctly remembers a most unusual happening:

During the day, when we were meeting some members of the Indian community, I was informed that one of the most prominent members of the Hindu community, a gentleman with a bad heart, would attend our programme that evening. This gentleman liked to play the *tabla*. I didn’t have the opportunity to talk to him much before the programme, but I did see him at the front of the audience, sitting with his family. Our Gaura-Nitāi Deities were on a palanquin on the stage. I led the *kīrtana* as we circumambulated Śrī Śrī Gaura-Nitāi. I could see that this gentleman was becoming very enthusiastic and with a very beautiful smile he came up and placed his *tabla* on the stage.

It came to me, as I looked at him, that this man was going to die if we continued, and yet I could see in his face that he wanted to leave his body, hearing and chanting the name of the Lord. I had the choice to stop the *kīrtana* and maybe save him, or to continue the *kīrtana* and satisfy his yearning. As we circumambulated and chanted, he was playing his *tabla* passionately with a blissful smile on his face. As I looked on, he fell down at my feet. He was dead.

As the *kīrtana* continued, members of the audience came up to the stage and took his body to a room where his doctor was waiting. The doctor tried injecting adrenaline into his heart, but it was too late. We can reflect that this was a very fortunate soul indeed. He had wholeheartedly embraced the holy name and given up his body in that divine mood. This man was obviously a very pious and great soul, blessed by the holy names and taken back to Godhead. He was absorbed in the holy name of the Lord and was in that spirit of complete surrender when he died. The audience was very sombre, because this man was very much loved in the Hindu community.

The following day the man’s cremation took place on a wood fire, and in the presence of his family I officiated and said some prayers. I can see him now in my mind’s eye with that very blissful smile on his face.

Puṣṭa Kṛṣṇa Swami was careful to ensure that the philosophy of Kṛṣṇa consciousness was always presented with honesty and sincerity. He knew these qualities to be the right ingredients for the successful foundation of ISKCON in South Africa. Insisting that devotees wear devotional clothing at all times, he was very sensitive to what others might think and feel, especially as the devotees were emissaries of one of the world’s most ancient cultures. Wherever and whenever the devotees went preaching and distributing books, the people should be able to recognise that they were monks.

\* \* \*

Towards the end of 1974, after the long haul through South Africa, the exhausted devotees made a visit to a very pious person, Mr Natoo R. Patel, who offered the devotees a room to stay in at his home near Pretoria. For the time being, this was the only place they had. Mr Patel stayed in the Indian township of Laudium at 243 Aquamarine Street, which was their base for the next month. He was well acquainted with the philosophy of Kṛṣṇa consciousness; indeed, he had his own cultural mission called the Hare Krishna Bhajan Group.

Owing to his dedication, he managed to help Puṣṭa Kṛṣṇa Swami and the devotees arrange programmes at the Hindu Seva Samaj Temple. This resulted in the devotees making many life-members during their stay. His wife, Mrs Manooben Patel, cooked for the devotees throughout their stay.

During their stay in Pretoria, they had the opportunity to establish a permanent temple, so Jagat Guru and Puṣṭa Kṛṣṇa ventured into Johannesburg in search of a suitable base. One day, in a nice quiet suburb of Johannesburg called Yeoville, they found a modest detached house. It was 59 Muller Street. This was ideal for the new temple.

Śrīla Prabhupāda was well aware that Indians would not join so easily at that time because of their rigid family system, and in a letter to Puṣṭa Kṛṣṇa, dated 16th October 1974 from Māyāpura, he openly encouraged Europeans to come forward:

Your success with the South Africans there is very good. You must get more South Africans to join and make our mission solid there, so get the Europeans to join, as many as possible. Regarding the draft situation, let them go from the college to the temple. They can stay with us for some time and then go back and forth. In the colleges they can introduce our books. We are getting very good written appreciation from learned circles in USA of our books, and many libraries and universities are ordering an open order for all of the books as they are published.

Regarding the book distribution to the Indians: yes, that was my programme. Hold a meeting and sell books. These books are not fiction or imagination. If the Indians like the authority of the books, take advantage and distribute profusely.

By turning many westerners into followers of Kṛṣṇa consciousness, Prabhupāda gave the Indians self-confidence in their own culture, *sanātana-dharma*. This was Śrīla Prabhupāda’s expertise in reminding the Indians of their roots in Vedic culture.

## In Separation

Chapter 4  
In Separation

In March 1975, Puṣṭa Kṛṣṇa Swami travelled to India for the Mayapura festival. I was also on my way to India from America as a book distributor with the BBT Airport Sankirtan Party.

In Mayapura I came in contact with Bhāgavata dāsa, who was intent on putting me in touch with Puṣṭa Kṛṣṇa Swami. When Bhāgavata dāsa heard I was born in Durban, he immediately led me to Puṣṭa Kṛṣṇa Swami for recruitment into his zone. Because I was a successful book distributor, Bhāgavata knew that I would be invaluable in South Africa.

At our first meeting, Puṣṭa Kṛṣṇa Swami had told me, “One day, Roger, I will take you to South Africa.” I was taken aback by this. Puṣṭa Kṛṣṇa asked me whether I would like to go to South Africa and help with the book distribution and preaching there. Naturally I was quite excited by this offer. Before I could go I had to gain the approval of my authority, Tripurāri dāsa, the leader of our BBT travelling party. At first Tripurāri was reluctant to comply with Puṣṭa Kṛṣṇa Swami’s request because I was amongst his best *saṅkīrtana* men and he did not want to lose me. I thought I would not be able to go.

One week later, however, while in Vṛndāvana, after having received *sannyāsa* initiation from Śrīla Prabhupāda, the now Swami Tripurāri finally conceded that it would be a great service opportunity for me to travel to South Africa as a BBT *saṅkīrtana* representative, so that I could train other distributors and increase hardback book distribution. I was happy that Tripurāri Swami had let me go, for this was also the mood of Prabhupāda in that he wanted his experienced disciples to expand their service and train others all over the world. In one way I was sad to be leaving the BBT Airport Sankirtan Party as it was a very special part of my life.

Before leaving Delhi *en route* to South Africa, Puṣṭa Kṛṣṇa Swami and I went to the Ministry of External Affairs. Puṣṭa Kṛṣṇa persevered to get permission for the clearance and the endorsement necessary for Śrīla Prabhupāda to visit South Africa. In his gentle and diplomatic way, he persuaded the officials to grant the no-objection endorsement still required from the Indian Government to allow Śrīla Prabhupāda to visit South Africa. In India such things took a lot of time and patience; after all, India had condemned the apartheid system.

Śrīla Prabhupāda’s Indian passport, page seven, had the following text endorsed in it: *Also Valid For Travel to*: *All countries in Africa (excluding Rhodesia, South Africa and Portuguese Possessions).*

Numerous restrictive endorsements by the Indian government had to be repeated in Prabhupāda’s passport, which simply delayed his trip to South Africa several times. In this way it seemed that India was boycotting South Africa by its handling of the affair. In addition, it was even more difficult for an Indian to acquire a South African visa. There was no South African mission in India, so the application had to be processed at the notorious Ministry of the Department of the Interior, located in Pretoria. Even after all this, there was no guarantee of obtaining a visa. When all was said and done, His Divine Grace’s application had no priority owing to the fact that it was in the hands of racially motivated officials.

\* \* \*

Puṣṭa Kṛṣṇa Swami and I made our way to London. It was less expensive and more convenient for us to fly from there to South Africa. As I still had my return ticket from Delhi to New York via London, I simply used the remaining portion of the ticket to get to London. We spent a few weeks at Bhaktivedanta Manor near London, arranging tickets and preparing for our journey to South Africa.

On reflection, during 1972 I had little or no direct contact with His Divine Grace. However, some physical separation between the disciple and the spiritual master can often be a blessing in disguise. I remember, shortly after I joined ISKCON in Vancouver, one week in June during the annual Rose Festival, all the devotees in the temple went to Eugene, a big college town in the Oregon State, to attend a series of lectures given by Śrīla Prabhupāda.

Unfortunately I was instructed to stay behind and take care of the Vancouver temple. Although they wanted someone to look after the place, it was for my own benefit. If I were to have seen Śrīla Prabhupāda prematurely, I may well have considered him to be an ordinary person, and in so doing committed offences that would have stunted my spiritual life. It was to be almost a year and a half before I would see him in person.

In retrospect, I can see that I had to prepare myself to become spiritually mature and to build up a service attitude. Being a newly initiated disciple of Śrīla Prabhupāda did not prevent me from communicating with him. I had written to Śrīla Prabhupāda with gratitude for accepting me as his disciple and I had sent him a small *dakṣiṇā* of US $108 in 1972.

One quiet moment during my early days in the movement in 1972, whilst meditating on a picture of Śrīla Prabhupāda I perceived tears flowing from his eyes. It looked as though he had come out of the photograph and was there before me with tears flowing down his face. This happened while I was in deep meditation, privately offering *pūjā.*

This experience I had was by his great compassion. He was telling me in a subtle way that I was his son. The experience was very reassuring. I could understand that His Divine Grace’s presence should never be minimised. The whole experience made me feel humbled and peaceful. I knew that he was always conscious of his followers. That experience I shall never forget. Indeed, it reminds me that Śrīla Prabhupāda is never far away.

Then on another occasion in August 1973, while travelling in America, I decided that the separation was too much. In an act of love for my spiritual master, I requested my parents, who at that time had settled in London, to send me a return air ticket for my journey. I had just learnt that the musician George Harrison (former Beatle) had given the movement the use of an English country mansion on the outskirts of London, and that Śrīla Prabhupāda was there.

Within a day of conceiving the whole idea, I flew to London. On arrival at Heathrow Airport, I met Śrīla Prabhupāda’s long-time secretary, Śyāmasundara dāsa, who was waiting there for other leading devotees. He greeted me and gave directions to drive to Bhaktivedanta Manor. My mother and I came across the Manor in a quiet countryside location in Hertfordshire near North London. We drove to Radlett, stopped at a flower shop and purchased a few dozen roses. We then proceeded a little further to Letchmore Heath and the English country home.

Arriving at the Manor, I immediately sought out the sewing room and asked my mother to prepare a flower garland. A devotee showed her how to make a rose garland. Just as we had finished making the garland, I remember looking up at the grand staircase from the temple-room and seeing the angelic figure of Śrīla Prabhupāda, which appeared to be floating down the stairs.

He was enshrined in a wonderful, golden aura emanating from his entire body. As he entered the temple-room, my mother and I observed him closely. He indeed carried the authority of someone magnanimous. He was like an emperor and had that heavy, spiritual presence. This was the first time I had seen His Divine Grace in person, and as he walked towards the newly installed Deities, Śrī Śrī Rādhā-Gokulānanda, I realised I had never before seen such an effulgent person.

It was as if he was contained in a bubble of spiritual energy like an angel. After Śrīla Prabhupāda offered his prostrated obeisances to the Deities, Śrī Śrī Rādhā-Gokulānanda, he swiftly made his way to the *vyāsāsana*, the seat of the spiritual master.

It was his Vyāsa-pūjā, the appearance day of the spiritual master. One hundred and eight preparations had been cooked by senior disciples Kṣīrodakaśāyī Viṣṇu dāsa, his wife Kīrtīma devī dāsī and their three sons. Their act of devotion was exemplary.

In the temple-room the Vyāsa-pūjā began, and the devotees appeared awestruck in the presence of Śrīla Prabhupāda. As the chanting became brisker, I was enlivened to dance in front of my spiritual master. One by one the devotees went up and offered flower petals at the feet of His Divine Grace. I humbly waited my turn and then took the garland from my mother and offered it to Śrīla Prabhupāda. I felt my mission was accomplished as there could not have been a better day to do it in. Feeling great satisfaction, I began attending Śrīla Prabhupāda’s lectures daily, an experience that I shall never forget for the rest of my life. It was then I understood that the separation had been worthwhile. My mother and father had done a great service in sending me the air ticket. Without their help, I could not have experienced this sublime part of my life.

These fond memories gave me the strength and determination to continue my spiritual journey, and to prepare for South Africa in the years to come.

## Moving into Top Gear

Chapter 5  
Moving into Top Gear

Now at last we were ready to fly to Johannesburg. This was the first time I had entered South Africa to reside since I left with my parents in 1954, when they quit the country because of the progressive implementation of the apartheid policy. Having faith in Śrīla Prabhupāda, I had no fear of what lay ahead. In fact I was quite excited about my new assignment. We departed for Jan Smuts Airport in Johannesburg on 20th May 1975.

Being born in South Africa, I was allowed to go straight to the counter reserved for indigenous nationals. It was a strange experience for me since apartheid was in full swing and technically, by the criteria of the apartheid regime, I was a non-White. Puṣṭa Kṛṣṇa Swami had advised me to dress smartly so as not to cause problems. At the counter I showed my passport and birth certificate. When the official saw that I was born in Durban, I was allowed into the country without any restrictions.

We soon arrived at the Johannesburg temple. It was a small detached house in a suburb called Yeoville, an area mostly occupied by middle-class Jewish people. It had a well-kept lawn and gardens, front and rear, and several fruit trees including a nice fig tree. There was also a garage used as a storeroom for the books. The rent was about R150 per month. In the newly-acquired house, we had the temple-room completely soundproofed so that our neighbours would not be disturbed by the early morning *kīrtanas*.

I met all the devotees, including Gokulendra, Pārtha-sārathi, Dhīrāṅga from England, Rāmānujācārya from our centre in Nairobi, and Rocanī dāsī. We were all *brahmacārīs* except for Puṣṭa Kṛṣṇa Swami, who was a *sannyāsī*, and Rocanī dāsī, the only *brahmacāriṇī* (female devotee) present. She was given separate accommodation and was treated with the utmost respect. It was a nice homely atmosphere. Rocanī dāsī was academically inclined and had expertly translated a number of Śrīla Prabhupāda’s smaller books into the Afrikaans language. Although Rocanī was often in a lot of pain, owing to a disability, she always managed to smile and had a peaceful nature.

\* \* \*

The preaching opportunities in the Johannesburg area proved to be ripe. All the Sunday Love Feasts were well-attended, with young people coming forward demonstrating their attraction and devotion to Lord Śrī Kṛṣṇa, coupled with an interest in the philosophy. Many of the regular guests began to carry out Kṛṣṇa consciousness in their own homes, and in this way the process was taking effect.

Within a short time I began distributing Śrīla Prabhupāda’s books. I demonstrated to the devotees the ease with which it was possible to distribute large quantities of hardback *Bhagavad-gītā As It Is, Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam* and *Kṛṣṇa*, *the Supreme Personality of Godhead*. I first ventured out to a cosmopolitan area close to the temple called Hillbrow, which reminded me of somewhere in Italy. At night, however, it was transformed into a bustling shopping area with nightclubs, casinos, theatres, expensive boutiques and dozens of restaurants. It was full of wealthy people out to enjoy the atmosphere. I could see it was a perfect situation for distributing hundreds of books and magazines and making our mark on the local community.

\* \* \*

The American devotee Rāmānujācārya dāsa Brahmacārya had come from the ISKCON Nairobi temple in February 1974. An expert *mṛdaṅga* player and a good cook, he was strongly brahminical and a definite asset to the South African *yātrā.*

Pārtha-sārathi, Rāmānujācārya, Dhīraṅga and Gokulendra were all engaged in enrolling life-members. Pārtha-sārathi and Rāmānujācārya really pushed the life-membership programme on, which took off in a big way. We were hardly known by the Hindus in Johannesburg then as it was a new field for us. Pārtha-sārathi in particular was expert at making life-members. Sometimes he was accompanied by Dhīrāṅga, who also had some success. Quite often Pārtha-sārathi would come back with two or three newly paid-up members. This encouraged us all.

Dhīraṅga, an intelligent English devotee, was responsible for sending out newsletters to our life-members. He also organised a substantial mailing list. Even before my arrival, there were at least a hundred members. However, the devotees had also made a point of going out to European sectors of Johannesburg. Śrīla Prabhupāda wanted the message of Kṛṣṇa consciousness to be available to them. Previously the local devotees had found it difficult to distribute large books amongst the Europeans. This was the main reason Puṣṭa Kṛṣṇa Swami had asked me to come to South Africa. I was concerned that book distribution in South Africa would be very different from that in the United States, but to my amazement it was just as productive if not more lucrative. I would take several boxes of books with me and distribute ten or twenty hardbacks on a good day. It was so nice because we could present the books on their own merit and be more relaxed in our approach to the public.

Fortunately our reputation was carefully protected by Puṣṭa Kṛṣṇa Swami, who insisted on honest and sincere behaviour at all times.

We gave out our books in crowded places such as shopping malls and parking lots. In April 1975 and throughout that year, I went out on the streets distributing books, especially at weekends when there were thousands of shoppers about. Because I was working so intensely, I had to wear ordinary clothes to minimise the risk of being harassed.

I can still clearly remember one incident that took place in Hillbrow. I had been distributing books that evening for a few hours and I must have collected at least one hundred rands.

A European businessman who had been observing my activities approached me and said, “By gosh, you are a good salesman!” He was an insurance representative and seemed impressed with me. He went on to say, “I want you as one of my salesmen to sell insurance. Here is my card. Contact me and I’ll hire you immediately.” Of course, I was not about to get a job, but I did feel a sense of worth that boosted my confidence. I continued my service.

Railway stations I especially liked because there were lots of Europeans coming and going. I totally ignored all signs saying “White area” or “non-White area.” There was one station for non-Whites and another for Whites. Once, as I was distributing books in the White area, a train pulled in packed with white South African military recruits. They had come from Boot Camp in Bloemfontein. As they came teeming off the train, I stopped them briefly and got them to donate something for Śrīla Prabhupāda’s books. Many gave up to R20 at a time. I would explain to them that the book was very valuable, that it was about meditation and peace of mind and so on. I was distributing big books at a rate of ten to twenty an hour. Surprisingly they were so approachable and very receptive and communicative. I was ecstatic!

Unbeknown to me, I was being observed by the secret police, who kept a keen eye on anyone distributing literature. That day one of them approached me and spoke with a strong Afrikaans accent. “I am from the police.” He showed me his official badge.

“Oh yes,” I said, somewhat nonchalantly. Since he had shown me his badge, which authorised him in his duties, I wanted to show him my authority so I showed him a copy of *Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam*. I opened the inside cover of the book and said, “Read this—eighteen thousand verses on the science of God! This is what I am distributing.”

He answered, “Oh yes. Very good.”

Then I said, “This is for you; I am presenting you with this book. Kindly take my invitation card. Why don’t you come along to our vegetarian love-feast programme on Sunday?”

At first he probably thought that I was a communist and was about to arrest me, but when I showed him what I was doing he calmed down. He took the book from me saying that he would take it to the police department to read, and then let me know what he thought of it.

To my amazement he came to the Sunday feast at the temple and was curious as to what was going on. I felt that he had not come out of duty as a policeman, but rather in a private capacity. I welcomed him and showed him around while explaining the philosophy. He took *prasādam* and made a donation for our famous vegetarian love feast and for the book which I had previously given him.

This was quite remarkable. It was unheard of for a member of the secret police to be so mellow. I strongly believe that once he had come in touch with the book Śrīla Prabhupāda’s words guided him. In those days it was quite possible for people like me, distributing literature that had even a hint of political flavour, to disappear mysteriously at any time. I knew that the words of Śrīla Prabhupāda in those books were protecting me. After that I stayed away from train stations and the military. I tried to follow what Puṣṭa Kṛṣṇa Swami always emphasised: to preach the philosophy as it was given by Śrīla Prabhupāda and to steer clear of politics altogether.

We were informed that whenever we showed our Hare Krishna films using an 8mm projector, we were being spied on. Quite often in fact we were surprised by plain-clothes detectives arriving at our door shortly after the film began. They must have thought we were showing communist propaganda. We heard that certain members of the congregation were spying on behalf of the secret police. I got the feeling that some of them were acting like friends, but were really informers. They pretended to be devotees and came regularly to our programmes. Strangely enough, when Śrīla Prabhupāda finally came in person they all seemed to vanish into thin air. Of course, we had nothing to hide and had complete faith in *guru* and Kṛṣṇa.

On Fridays and Saturdays I would go to the shopping malls like Eastgate, demonstrating to the devotees how to distribute books, and introducing myself to people as an international book rep. I felt empowered by Kṛṣṇa to quickly befriend people and in this way distribute books profusely. I would distribute twenty or even thirty hardbacks in half a day and come back with several hundred rands. In those days that was a very substantial sum of money.

On other occasions we would go to the Johannesburg Airport and sit down next to people showing them books, which they would often purchase. I would make friends with them in a similar fashion, although the security at the airport was much stricter. As a result, Europeans received large numbers of Śrīla Prabhupāda’s books. The most rewarding areas were downtown Pretoria and the Hillbrow streets on Saturdays, when all the devotees came out surcharged with the *saṅkīrtana* spirit.

\* \* \*

Pretoria was a very wealthy and predominantly Afrikaans-speaking city. Gokulendra, Pārtha-sārathi and I, along with the other devotees, would distribute large quantities of books to the mostly Afrikaner residents, who were favourable to our religious books and generous with donations. At that time, Rocanī received some money from a family inheritance and purchased a brand-new Volkswagen bus, which we used to commute to Pretoria. We were doing exceptionally well. The shipments were coming in and the books were going out.

Pārtha-sārathi became very expert in approaching Hindu businessmen and making them life-members. This was the first time that the Hindu community had been asked to give support to the small temple. ISKCON South Africa was at last a financially sound institution. From then on we focused on expanding so that we could become independent and serve as an example to the world at large. We respected Puṣṭa Kṛṣṇa Swami as our local temple president and leader. He set a good example and engendered the right mood by coming out to lead the chanting parties on a regular basis. We were inspired tremendously by this. He also had a unique way of distributing books.

On one occasion he told me how he approached the Central News Agency, the largest book outlet in South Africa, and had a meeting with the chief buyer, who was very impressed by Puṣṭa Kṛṣṇa appearing in traditional *svāmī’s* clothing and by the fact that he actually took the time to explain the contents of the books. He discussed the philosophy and established a friendly relationship with him. The buyer purchased one thousand rands’ worth of *Kṛṣṇa, the Supreme Personality of Godhead* (trilogy), which went on sale in their branches all over South Africa. Puṣṭa Kṛṣṇa Swami was very charismatic irrespective of whether he was giving lectures, meeting people, managing the temple or guiding the devotees.

I felt that most South Africans had a positive, respectful approach to religious people. And on that basis, South Africa was a book distributor’s paradise.

\* \* \*

It was about this time that two Canadian devotees, Rukma dāsa and Viṣṇudatta dāsa, came from the ISKCON Nairobi temple. The younger one, Viṣṇudatta (who was about eighteen), was an expert musician who could play *mṛdaṅga* beautifully. He was also a good speaker. Rukma dāsa, a devotee of long standing, had joined in 1970. He was an expert cook and a skilful book distributor.

Also at this time we heard that Śrīla Prabhupāda was making firm plans to travel to South Africa. Śrīla Prabhupāda wrote to Puṣṭa Kṛṣṇa Swami from Honolulu on 15th June 1975:

Please accept my blessings. I am in due receipt of your letter dated June 11th 1975 and have noted the contents. I am prepared to come to South Africa by the middle of July. I can come there from Brazil. We shall need at least one visa for Wayne Gunderson (Upendra) and, if possible, one for Robert Campagnola (Harikeśa). Please arrange for that. I hope this meets you in good health.

Śrīla Prabhupāda always intended to visit South Africa. Since the very beginning of the Hare Kṛṣṇa movement in South Africa, seemingly insurmountable obstacles had blocked the path for his intended visit. The situation differed from that in the rest of the world because the whole country was politically isolated.

Nevertheless, by the mercy of Śrīla Prabhupāda, I could feel his presence quite strongly, regardless of his physical absence. When we knew that Śrīla Prabhupāda was finalising his travel arrangements, the devotees shifted into top gear. Viṣṇudatta prepared the artwork for the posters announcing his arrival. Plans were made to take Śrīla Prabhupāda on tour in South Africa. Everyone was very excited. The finished posters were beautiful. In a photograph chosen by Puṣṭa Kṛṣṇa Swami, Prabhupāda had the look of one deep in trance. He seemed to be completely absorbed in love of Kṛṣṇa.

Since there were no computer graphics in those days, Viṣṇudatta did the artwork manually using Letraset rub on typographic lettering, which was then sent for printing. However, as it turned out, Śrīla Prabhupāda would not be setting foot on South African soil until several months later. To make matters more uncertain, a Delhi travel agent and scaremonger was spreading a rumour on the risks involved in going to South Africa. Somehow this was brought to Śrīla Prabhupāda’s attention, and in a letter to Puṣṭa Kṛṣṇa, there was a note about this strange story as follows:12

One travel agent in New Delhi has told us that two of her (American) clients were going to Johannesburg with visa, sufficient funds, onward ticket, passport, etc., and due to some sudden change in the government they were thrown into jail, and the next day they were sent at their own expense out of the country. The travel agent said that it could be even more severe for an Indian citizen, so if there is any risk it is not a very good idea. So, you should find out if there \[is\] any risk at all and then let me know at my Los Angeles address.

\* \* \*

During the next few months, we were hopeful of his visit, but still continued preaching. We visited the University of Witwatersrand in Johannesburg on a regular basis, where Puṣṭa Kṛṣṇa Swami gave very learned lectures. During break times, we held a short programme and the devotees would befriend the students. Puṣṭa Kṛṣṇa Swami would present Kṛṣṇa consciousness philosophy directly. We had very melodious *kīrtanas* and I followed up by selling books. We gave out *prasādam* freely to everyone who came along.

One of the most memorable university programmes took place during a regular visit to Natal at Durban- Westville University, where the rector, Professor Olivier, who was favourable to us, had arranged for one thousand students to attend a lecture given by Puṣṭa Kṛṣṇa Swami. This lecture was a dynamic success as Swami presented the Kṛṣṇa consciousness philosophy in a very palatable way, while appealing to the students’ scholarly way of thinking.

Every now and then, we would get a shipment of books from the BBT in Los Angeles. We would drive to Durban and arrange to clear the shipment, but the consignments of books were so large we could not take all the stock in one go. We therefore used to store a lot of the books at Mr Damji Kara’s garage in Durban. The rest of the stock would be brought by our Combie van and stored at the main Johannesburg temple. In that way we had stock at both locations. We drove down regularly to Durban to maintain our links with the Indian community there.

Jivan Naidoo was one of the first Indian South Africans to stay with the devotees at La-Mercy Beach and at the Surat Hindu Association Buildings in Durban. Jivan always went with us, assisting in street *hari-nāma* and book distribution in Chatsworth, the Indian suburb of Durban. He rendered practical help in unlimited ways and travelled to Johannesburg to help us on several occasions.

Whenever we were in Durban, we made contact with Mr J.T. Bhoola. He was very helpful and would arrange for our accommodation at the Surat Hindu Buildings in Victoria Street, Durban. The books previously stored were easily retrievable; we would simply drive down to Mr Kara’s private residence in Overport to collect the books we needed from his garage.

However, at that time we concentrated most of our efforts in the City of Johannesburg. I encouraged public book distribution wherever there were Europeans. The devotees distributed *Kṛṣṇa*, *the Supreme Personality of Godhead* (trilogy), *The Perfection of Yoga*, *Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam*, and *Back to Godhead* magazines. Rukma and Viṣṇudatta also became quite expert at book distribution.

\* \* \*

Puṣṭa Kṛṣṇa Swami knew only too well the risks we were taking in trying to establish the movement. News of a fascist attack on an ISKCON centre in West Germany had filtered through to the South African media and had been published in a unfavourable light in a popular magazine.

Puṣṭa Kṛṣṇa Swami immediately wrote to the magazine explaining ISKCON’s aims and objectives, stating that it was a genuine religion stemming from an authentic Vedic culture. The magazine replied, thanking him for his explanation and apologising for their mistake, which had arisen from the author’s misunderstanding of ISKCON’s philosophy. This episode proved Puṣṭa Kṛṣṇa Swami’s ability in defending Kṛṣṇa consciousness when needed.

Another clear example portrays his careful dealings. When a young South African teenager came to seek refuge in spiritual life, Puṣṭa Kṛṣṇa Swami felt unsure of her motives. After much consideration and many meetings with her concerned parents, he decided that it was in the girl’s best interest to remain at her parents’ home for the time being.

Puṣṭa Kṛṣṇa recalls:

The girl in question was South African of Jewish descent, who had joined in Johannesburg when she was under age. Her parents, who were orthodox Jews, came to me and we had many conversations. I felt that she had really come not for Kṛṣṇa consciousness, but to get away from her family. I thought this to be a wrong motive. I told her that if she was sincere she could still come and join when she had come of age, but she did not want to do that. I felt that her motivation was not necessarily pure. I did not stop her parents taking her out of the temple.

\* \* \*

Puṣṭa Kṛṣṇa desperately wanted to fulfil his spiritual master’s wishes. He wanted Śrīla Prabhupāda to come to South Africa in person. However, bringing him to South Africa was not an easy task, as he was about to find out. The first attempt to procure a visa for Śrīla Prabhupāda proved to be difficult. Prabhupāda’s application was a lengthy procedure, but this did not deter Puṣṭa Kṛṣṇa Swami at all.

Mr Rasikbhai C.I. Parekh, a Gujarati member of the Hindu Community in Johannesburg, had a strong desire to invite Śrīla Prabhupāda and facilitate ISKCON in South Africa. Because many Indians were drifting away from their culture, his support gave much-needed confidence to the Hindu community in South Africa.

Although Śrīla Prabhupāda’s application had been refused once already, the application was now being helped along by our friend of the temple, Mr Rasikbhai C.I. Parekh. He lived at 4410 Poppy Street in Lenasia, an Indian township which was a few hours drive from Johannesburg.

Unbeknown to us at the time, Mr Parekh was an active supporter of the African National Congress and of Nelson Mandela. Puṣṭa Kṛṣṇa relates the last stumbling blocks on the long road to obtaining the visa:

When we had finally secured a visa from the South African Government in Pretoria for Śrīla Prabhupāda, we still had the problem of getting Śrīla Prabhupāda’s Indian passport re-endorsed again so that he could visit South Africa. The Indian Government rejected Śrīla Prabhupāda’s request on several occasions and it seemed almost hopeless. There was also a deadline on the use of the South African visa for Śrīla Prabhupāda’s visit and it was running out.

I wrote a letter to Śrīla Prabhupāda during that time and he replied on 6th December 1974. A drama was unfolding. I felt that if Śrīla Prabhupāda were not able to come on this visa he would never be able to come. It had taken several years to secure that visa.

I also had to negotiate with Śrīla Prabhupāda’s personal secretaries, who were often changing. They had their own agendas for Śrīla Prabhupāda, which did not include South Africa because they themselves did not have visas to enter South Africa.

The whole matter was put before Kṛṣṇa, and my most profound prayer to the Supreme Lord was that he be able to manifest the desire of Śrīla Prabhupāda to place his lotus feet upon this land and bless these people. Their Indian culture had been suppressed systematically by government policies.

Śrīla Prabhupāda had been a follower of Gandhi when he was a young man, and I sometimes thought that as Gandhi had done so much to enliven the Indian population of South Africa so many years before, Śrīla Prabhupāda would create a renaissance for all the people of South Africa.

Śrīla Prabhupāda finally obtained a valid visa, but with restrictions:

1\. The visit could not exceed six weeks.

2\. Return ticket to be held.

3\. Re-entry to India to be assured.

4\. Satisfactory X-ray and medical reports to be held.

5\. Passport to be valid for travel to South Africa, and a deposit of R500 to be held at the Department of the Interior.

In a letter from Vṛndāvana, Śrīla Prabhupāda confirmed the approximate date of his visit: 13

N.B. Now I have decided that I must come to Africa. So my visa for South Africa is valid up to October 18th. Therefore I shall leave from Bombay approximately October 1st for Mauritius and stay there for one week. Puṣṭa Kṛṣṇa Swami may come there and take me to South Africa since none of my servants have South African visas. I shall stay in South Africa for one week. Then Puṣṭa Kṛṣṇa Swami may take me to Nairobi and I will stay there for one week and go to Mombassa if you think. Then I shall return to Bombay by the end of October. So you arrange this programme.

\* \* \*

With very little time left before Śrīla Prabhupāda’s arrival, Puṣṭa Kṛṣṇa Swami finally managed to arrange for the International Society for Krishna Consciousness to be registered as a charity in Pretoria. He did so after many months of haggling. The painful business of dealing with officials had to be handled with the greatest care.

There is an interesting story about how the society was registered. Gokulendra accompanied by Puṣṭa Kṛṣṇa Swami went to the charities registration department, located at the government buildings in Pretoria. All applications and applicants were heavily scrutinised by government officials since those were the days of apartheid, yet Gokulendra registered our society as if it were a European society.

The officials viewed Gokulendra as a typical member of a white South African society. Their point of view was that they wanted it to remain a white movement because no non-Whites had come forward at that time, although many Indians were invaluable helpers of ISKCON. The registrars felt better keeping us as a white South African society. It would be difficult to change their minds.

Feeling that he needed all the help he could muster and considering the rigid mentality of the people he had to deal with, Gokulendra took some *prasādam* for the registrars in the hope of having the opportunity to give them some. On so many occasions, *prasādam* had almost magically shown its effect on people. He had to explain the necessity of naming the society the “International Society for Krishna Consciousness” as the movement was known by this title throughout the world.

The registrar said, “Sorry, it can’t be the ‘International Society for Krishna Consciousness’—it has to be the ‘Society for Krishna Consciousness in South Africa.’ ” The government registrars were very wary about unusual international organisations coming into the country. As worldwide societies they might enable people of all races to come together in South Africa. This was their fear.

Gokulendra protested, “But we’re known by this name all over the world. We are an international society.”

The registrar said, “Sorry, it has to be the ‘Society for Krishna Consciousness in South Africa.’ ”

Just then his secretary came in with tea and biscuits, and when he was not looking Gokulendra slipped some Simply Wonderful sweets onto his plate. While he was drinking his tea, he ate a biscuit and looked at the *prasādam* sweets. He picked one up and took a bite, and as he swallowed it he turned to his secretary and said, “Give them their name.”

So in this way, we were registered in South Africa as the “International Society for Krishna Consciousness!”

## Śrīla Prabhupāda Finally Arrives

Chapter 6  
Śrīla Prabhupāda Finally Arrives

## Mauritius, late September 1975

Mauritius, late September 1975

By the time confirmation came that Śrīla Prabhupāda’s formalities for South Africa were completed, it was nearing the end of September 1975. Puṣṭa Kṛṣṇa Swami immediately began preparations to accompany Prabhupāda. Puṣṭa Kṛṣṇa remembers:

I flew to Mauritius to meet Śrīla Prabhupāda and then escort him into South Africa. Brahmānanda Mahārāja was Śrīla Prabhupāda’s personal secretary at the time. He was prohibited from ever entering South Africa because of his previous attempt to enter overland, years before, in his travelling *saṅkīrtana* safari van at the border with Rhodesia. Śrīla Prabhupāda said that I would be his secretary during his visit to South Africa. I developed a very intimate and even more affectionate relationship with Śrīla Prabhupāda during that visit. Later, when I arrived at the Māyāpura festival in India in the following months, Śrīla Prabhupāda asked me to become his permanent secretary.

Śrīla Prabhupāda would often call me in to chat with him at all times of the day or night, philosophising, talking business or bouncing his thoughts off me, as one would with a very close or intimate associate. Although I was unqualified in every matter, Śrīla Prabhupāda accepted me as one of his close and confidential associates. This began with his visit to South Africa.

The task of preparing for Śrīla Prabhupāda’s arrival in Durban was assigned to Gokulendra and myself. For the next two weeks we spent all our time making the necessary preparations. Meanwhile, back in Johannesburg at our Yeoville temple, Pārtha-sārathi was overseeing the construction of the *vyāsāsana*. The remaining devotees continued to keep the temple open for regular programmes.

\* \* \*

The posters advertising Śrīla Prabhupāda’s arrival, prepared some months back, were being posted in prominent locations throughout Johannesburg and Durban. Once the preparatory work was under way, Puṣṭa Kṛṣṇa Swami flew to meet Śrīla Prabhupāda in Mauritius. When he arrived, he realised that Śrīla Prabhupāda had to have a yellow fever injection to comply with South African health regulations. Travelling with Śrīla Prabhupāda at the time was Harikeśa dāsa Brahmacārī, his personal servant. Harikeśa distinctly remembers the occasion as follows:

We had decided that so as not to inconvenience Śrīla Prabhupāda Puṣṭa Kṛṣṇa would take the “jab” himself in place of Prabhupāda. To do this, Puṣṭa Kṛṣṇa Swami and I went to the inoculation centre in downtown Port Louis.

The inoculation centre was an old, run-down building with big columns around it. There was one room inside with rows of wooden benches, almost like a church, where lots of people were waiting to receive their injections. People were called up one at a time by name and would stand in front of everybody, take their injection and certificate and then leave.

When the officer eventually called out, “A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami,” Puṣṭa Kṛṣṇa went up to receive the injection. The official asked him, “What is your date of birth?”

“I don’t remember,” he said unconvincingly.

“What do you mean, you don’t remember? ” the officer said.

Puṣṭa Kṛṣṇa repeated, “I don’t remember. ”

At this, the people in the room began to laugh, thinking, “What is this nonsense person? He can’t remember his date of birth? ”

Sitting in the waiting room, the only thing I could think of doing was to start the congregational chanting of Hare Kṛṣṇa then and there, and wave my arms about. Eventually other people in the room started joining in. Puṣṭa Kṛṣṇa Swami meanwhile was trying to convince the officer. “Just give me the card and let me fill it in later when I remember my date of birth, ” he suggested.

For some reason, maybe because of the uproar in the room, the officer actually gave the card to him. Puṣṭa Kṛṣṇa Swami took the card and we both left, laughing our heads off. We filled in Prabhupāda’s birth date as 1st September 1896. This was incredible. We told Śrīla Prabhupāda this story later and he laughed as well.

The day that they all left for South Africa, Puṣṭa Kṛṣṇa Swami casually remarked to Śrīla Prabhupāda that he would change into ordinary clothes before leaving for South Africa.

Śrīla Prabhupāda seriously inquired whether he should also conform. He said, “So I will wear ordinary clothing as well if need be.” As it happened, Puṣṭa Kṛṣṇa decided that it was not necessary. Śrīla Prabhupāda was taking the humble position, being willing to conform to serve Kṛṣṇa in any way that could help. Back in South Africa, Gokulendra and I were alone in Durban making arrangements for the forthcoming programmes.

Fortunately, Mr J.T. Bhoola, our prominent life-member, arranged for a house in Westville to be made available for Śrīla Prabhupāda in a quiet, well-to-do suburb of Durban on the border between the European and Indian areas. It was a detached house located at 97 Devon Terrace, Westville, with big front and rear gardens and several large rooms. The good thing about this house was that it was only a few minutes’ drive from the town centre. The house belonged to “Chotubhai,” Naik, an associate of Mr Bhoola. As soon as we got the keys for the house, Gokulendra and I moved in and began the task of transforming the house into a residence with a spiritual atmosphere.

\* \* \*

Mr J.T. Bhoola, a discreet and highly successful Hindu entrepreneur, was the main person behind the scenes. He was well known for being a great supporter of Vedic culture. His name carried a lot of weight when it came to making the arrangements necessary to secure Durban City Hall for Śrīla Prabhupāda’s lectures, notwithstanding its contingent of white hardline bureaucrats.

During the seventies, when South Africa was still in the grip of the apartheid regime, it was almost impossible for the non-white population to do anything of real significance. The Europeans were given all kinds of privileges, and unless non-Whites had influence they were treated as second-class citizens and overlooked.

Our dear friend Mr J.T. Bhoola, who, in spite of being designated as an Indian, had a remarkable ability to pull a few strings, managed to get the hall booked for that night.

Before leaving us to accompany Śrīla Prabhupāda from Mauritius, Puṣṭa Kṛṣṇa made it clear that I was to officially co-ordinate Śrīla Prabhupāda’s arrival. He instructed that I should be the one to receive Śrīla Prabhupāda at the airport, warning me that the officials could become agitated if they were to see Whites bowing down before an Indian. Puṣṭa Kṛṣṇa was always very careful about public relations in South Africa. This was a blessing for me—the chance of a lifetime—and the perfect opportunity to honour my spiritual master, His Divine Grace Śrīla Prabhupāda.

I was given strict instructions to avoid using a luxurious car for Śrīla Prabhupāda as this might give the wrong impression. I found this instruction to be almost impossible to follow. I thought it over-cautious—this was my spiritual master arriving, and I wanted the best for him. As it happened, I tried to hire that most prestigious of cars, a Rolls-Royce, but there was none available. They were fully booked up. Even so, I could not get it out of my mind that I had to find a prestigious vehicle to honour Śrīla Prabhupāda on this his first visit to South Africa.
