Within a short time of its opening, the Hare Krishna temple became of considerable interest to many politicians. Nelson R. Mandela visited the temple on several occasions, firstly in 1992, on behalf of the African National Congress, not long after his historic release from prison, then again on 6th November 1994 for the Diwali festival as State President.

Upon entering the temple, Nelson Mandela humbly paid his respects by bowing down to the life-size *mūrti* of its founder, His Divine Grace A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami Prabhupāda.

President Mandela’s Diwali speech was followed by a speech given by His Holiness Bhakti-tīrtha Swami, which so moved the President that he stood up, applauded and embraced His Holiness. To conclude his three-hour visit, Nelson Mandela enjoyed a sanctified vegetarian banquet and stayed much longer than planned. He had cancelled all other engagements to be at the Hare Krishna temple.

## Highlights of Significant Events

Chapter 12  
**Highlights of Significant Events**

## Annual Ratha-yātrā festival

Annual Ratha-yātrā festival

From 1989 the Society was fortunate to have Indradyumna Swami come every year to South Africa to help organise the annual Ratha-yātrā festival. Over the years, the Festival of the Chariots has developed and is now held as one of the largest public gatherings (normally at holiday time) in the summer months. At first the festival was denied extensive news and television coverage, owing to apartheid and religious discrimination, but now, since the abolition of apartheid in the early nineties, it enjoys major press coverage and is regularly shown on television reports in South Africa.

In 1996 over 200,000 plates of free *prasādam* were distributed and three marquees were specially erected to answer the questions put by large numbers of the community.

The Durban Ratha-yātrā festivals are unique in that so many national leaders have actively participated and attended our functions. On 16th December 1995, I had attended a meeting with Deputy President Thabo Mbeki in his beachfront hotel room. Little did I know that Mr Mbeki was to be the special guest speaker on our stage at our Ratha-yātrā marquee later that day.

In his speech the Deputy President clearly stated: “Our country is in need of a lot of healing. It carries many scars and many wounds from the past. It carries many divisions. It needs that sense of oneness, which His Holiness \[Giriraj Swami\] has just been speaking about, a oneness, I believe, that is derived from a common conviction that as human beings we are all equal. All of us are entitled to our personal dignity and happiness, and all of us are equal in the eyes of God.

“You are people of peace, people who are committed to a non-violent world. His Holiness took me to the booth outside where cows are being protected, and said to me that this is also a soul and should not be made to suffer as a result of violence brought against it. It would be a very good thing if we all do something to end the violence in this province, and that we go out to preach this message of the International Society . . .”

After Deputy President Thabo Mbeki concluded his talk, he was presented with a copy of Śrīla Prabhupāda’s book, *Bhagavad-gītā As It Is.*

At one point, Mr Mbeki, accompanied by Giriraj Swami and Indradyumna Swami, toured the Ratha-yātrā Festival of the Chariots site. During the look around, Mr Mbeki stopped and began serving *prasādam* himself to an African child-guest, who was standing in line at the Hare Krishna Food for Life free *prasādam* booth*.*

\* \* \*

## Hare Kṛṣṇa Soweto

Hare Kṛṣṇa Soweto

Lord Caitanya predicted that Kṛṣṇa consciousness would spread to every town and village in the world. Latterly it has reached Soweto.

Soweto, the largest African township, situated ten kilometres outside Johannesburg, was the festering sore that burst through the skin of apartheid policies. Poverty, hunger, fear and violence haunted the inhabitants of Soweto. Burning in the fire of material existence, tormented by the threefold miseries, these suffering souls were ready to receive the benediction of Śrīla Prabhupāda’s mercy.

Prabhupāda was not satisfied only with delivering the Indian people, he also wanted to reach out to all ethnic groups, including the African peoples.

In South Africa, Indians and Africans were segregated. But since a Kṛṣṇa conscious person does not make any distinction based on the body, Prabhupāda said that the Indians had a duty to share their spiritual culture with the Africans. “It is an African country,” he said. “We should be preaching to them. And the programme should be simple: distributing *prasādam*, distributing free books and chanting Hare Kṛṣṇa with drums and *karatālas*.”

Thus, under the direction of Śrīla Prabhupāda’s expert instruction, a simple but effective preaching programme was devised for Soweto, initially consisting largely of mass spiritual-food distribution and congregational street-chanting. The programme was successful in attracting many interested persons to Kṛṣṇa consciousness. Having first-hand experience of the miseries of material existence, many were eager to receive a spiritual alternative.

Mahāprabhu dāsa and his wife, Mālinī dāsī, both joined Kṛṣṇa consciousness full-time. Their small three-room house soon became the first ISKCON preaching centre in Soweto. Using this as their base, they conducted weekly preaching programmes with *kīrtanas*, lectures and *prasādam* distribution. Attended regularly by a group of interested young teenagers, the programme slowly attracted more and more newcomers.

Mahāprabhu dāsa and Mālinā dāsī were among the very first African devotees to join ISKCON in South Africa. Despite the apartheid mentality at the time, in March 1987 they became full-time members in Muldersdrift. Prior to joining the temple, Mahāprabhu worked as an assistant supervisor for a commercial company.

He was born and grew up in Soweto, where he witnessed many of the horrors manifesting from the political turmoil in South Africa. After the Sharpeville Massacre, his father sent him to the homelands in Pietersburg for four years. That undoubtedly saved him.

When he returned home in 1980, the nefarious practice of “necklacing” had already begun. A burning rubber tyre was slung round the neck of a police informer, who was left to burn to death. Mahāprabhu witnessed many horrific scenes of violence and killing.

In 1984 his father died. His world, and that of his three sisters and three elder brothers, was suddenly turned upside down. Then, in his search for a solution to the problems in South Africa—maybe even a spiritual solution—Mahāprabhu became first a Muslim, then a Buddhist, and even a Hindu for a short time. A Hindu acquaintance suggested that he would fit in very well with the Hare Krishnas as he was “too inquisitive!”

His search still incomplete, he turned atheist. It was at this point that Mahāprabhu had the good fortune to meet a devotee named Bhakta Temba on the streets of Johannesburg in late August 1986. The devotee, who was staying at a temple farm near Muldersdrift, gave him a book, *The Science of Self-Realisation*. He read the book in a taxi, and by the time he arrived in Soweto he was already a convinced vegetarian, non-smoker and non-drinker.

In Mahāprabhu’s own words: “I opened the book and Prabhupāda was speaking directly on the regulative principles. I read through the night. Mālinī thought I was crazy.

“It was on the Saturday I read *The Science of Self-Realisation,* and on the Monday I left work to get more books. Once in town I met another devotee, Kunti dāsī, at the same place I had met Bhakta Temba. She supplied me with a copy of *Bhagavad-gītā As It Is*. I read it overnight and all my questions were answered.

“Kunti invited me to the first-ever Hare Kṛṣṇa programme arranged in Soweto that, by coincidence, was to be held that very day. The following Tuesday evening, two devotees, Giriraj Swami and Rūpa-ragunātha dāsa, held a programme at my house.

“The following Sunday I visited the temple with Mālinī. Then I started going at weekends. Rūpa-ragunātha began a weekly programme at our house on a Wednesday, after the ‘Food for Life’ distribution in Soweto. It was about this time that I stopped taking my job seriously, and because I was taking days off regularly, my boss kept telephoning me. Finally, Rūpa-ragunātha convinced me to join full time.”

After his son was born in January 1987, Mahāprabhu took up full-time service with the Food for Life programme. He would cook in Muldersdrift, distributing the *prasādam* in Soweto on Mondays, Wednesdays and Fridays. Saturdays were spent on *hari-nāma* in Johannesburg.

The Food for Life programme had been started by a female devotee, Ajita dāsī, and funded by a prominent European businessman, who made monthly donations of R2,500. This arrangement came about after a chance conversation they had on a flight from Germany in 1987.

A devotee named Viśākhā dāsī took over Food for Life later on. Then finally in 1991, when Viśākhā dāsī went to India, Mahāprabhu took over, doing the cooking and *prasādam* distribution himself, with the assistance of a devotee named Tribhaṅga-sundara dāsa.

The Johannesburg temple was now based in a six-storey building in Hillbrow, bordering the commercial downtown area of Johannesburg, with ample facility to reach out to the numerous homeless in the neighbourhood. Tribhaṅga-sundara, the temple president at the time, was inspired by the challenge of maintaining the newly-formed temple in Hillbrow, a challenge that was to become ever more demanding as time went on in the early nineties.

Food for Life distribution was performed three times a day: at schools in the morning, in town in the afternoon and at children’s homes in the evening. Mahāprabhu was generously supported by many members of the Indian congregation, who gave large donations of foodstuffs and equipment. All in all, R100,000 was spent on the Food for Life kitchen. Mālinī cooked regularly for the temple, doing breakfast, lunch and the Sunday Love Feast.

During this period from 1990 to 1997, the devotees rendered good service to the local Indian population, and numerous white visitors also came to take shelter of Śrī Kṛṣṇa in the temple. Unfortunately, Hillbrow soon degenerated into a slum. But in spite of this and the growing lawlessness of drugs gangs, the devotees resolutely carried out *saṅkīrtana* and Food for Life, feeding thousands daily. After a few years Hillbrow became a very dangerous place to live in, and by 1997 ISKCON had sold the six-storey building and moved out. Then finally the large white Gaura-Nitāi Deities were relocated to the Indian sector of Lenasia.

Mahāprabhu decided to move to Soweto so that he could preach more effectively in the African township. Thrice weekly programs were held on a Wednesday, Saturday and Sunday, with twenty or more people coming on a Sunday. Mahāprabhu had much success in recruiting devotees from amongst the youth of Soweto.

This humble endeavour on the part of the devotees was just the beginning of a large operation to spread Kṛṣṇa consciousness throughout Soweto. Thus, Kṛṣṇa consciousness has made its presence felt in Soweto, and has shown promise of some day transforming the suffering of its inhabitants into the ecstasy of devotional service to the Supreme Personality of Godhead, Śrī Kṛṣṇa.

\* \* \*

## Food for Life

Food for Life

*Prasādam* distribution is an essential function of ISKCON. Śrīla Prabhupāda, ISKCON’s founder and abiding authority, has specified that no one within a ten-mile radius of an ISKCON centre should go without *prasādam*, sanctified food.

Over 20,000 plates of *prasādam* are distributed yearly to universities and colleges in South Africa. Cape Town and Johannesburg ISKCON centres also have Food for Life kitchens and regularly distribute free *prasādam*.

To cap it all, Hare Krishna Food for Life organised a huge event, the Festival for the Children of the Rainbow Nation, in Durban on 23rd April 1997, at which 70,000 children from many different backgrounds were fed *prasādam*, which nourishes both body and soul. Of the 70,000 children, 37,000 were African, 20,000 Indian, 8,000 Coloured and 5,000 White.

President Nelson Mandela, special guest of honour, described the festival as “one of the happiest days of my life.” Other dignitaries present included KwaZulu Natal Premier, Dr Ben Ngubane, King Goodwill Zwelithini and the Mayor of Durban.

The festival’s director was Kapiladeva dāsa, who so expertly developed ISKCON’S *prasādam* distribution programme throughout the nineties in South Africa, when he was based at the Hare Kṛṣṇa temple in Chatsworth. He was thrilled when President Mandela accepted his invitation. In his speech the President said:

“Today’s festival \[was\] organised by the Hare Krishna Food for Life. On behalf of all of us here, I extend our warmest thanks for the kindness and dedication that you have shown by bringing us all together. Your goal of a hunger-free South Africa is one that is shared by the Government. It is central to the reconstruction and development of our country. Your efforts are making a practical contribution to this most urgent task.”

The full speech was reported in a national newspaper, *The Daily News*. The Festival for the Children of the Rainbow Nation received high commendation and was widely reported in South Africa’s national press. The whole event, which lasted some six hours, was covered by SABC, *Polish* TV, CNN and three other South African national television channels.

Headlines in the *Sowetan* read: MANDELA LAUDS HARE KRISHNA FOR HELPING KIDS.

*The Mercury* had this to say: “Mr Mandela said that the Hare Krishna movement’s goal of a hunger-free South Africa was one that was shared by the Government;” and that the festival was “the only one of its kind in the world.”

*The Daily News* in an article stated: “The \[Hare Krishna festival\] programme is an attempt to revive the ancient culture of hospitality by uniting people with food and spiritual education.”

And, finally, an in-depth report in *The Post* said it all:

“Politicians from President Mandela to ministers and parliamentarians have opened their hearts to the Hare Krishna movement’s unique Food for Life project that feeds almost one million hungry people in KwaZulu Natal each year.

“The region’s political personalities demonstrated their commitment to this world-beating feeding scheme when Premier Dr Ben Ngubane postponed a cabinet meeting to attend the Festival for the Children of the Rainbow Nation at King’s Park soccer stadium in Durban.

“Hundreds of \[Hare Krishna\] devotees cooked the biggest picnic lunch for children from schools, children’s homes \[and\] special educational institutions.

“Mr Mandela praised the organisers, led by its project director, Mr Kapiladeva, and chief sponsor Mr Allan Reddy, saying they have set an example for all South Africans of a caring and community spirit which transcends cultural barriers and allows us to look ahead to a peaceful and prosperous future for all our citizens.

“The midday picnic of dance and music, hailed as a major breakthrough for the Food for Life programme, would also go down as a watershed in the history of the International Society for Krishna Consciousness (ISKCON).

“Commented public-relations officer Champakalata dāsī, ‘The founder of the Hare Krishna movement, His Divine Grace Swami Prabhupāda, on his only visit to South Africa in 1975, expressed two desires for this country.

“ ‘Firstly, that a temple be built where people of all walks of life could come together and worship God.

“ ‘Secondly, the ancient Festival of Chariots be held on the Durban beachfront annually.’ ”

*The Post* article concluded: “Ten years later a majestic temple rose and turned Chatsworth’s skyline into a glorious sight; since then the Hare Krishna temple has not only become one of Durban’s tourist landmarks, but it has also developed into a spiritual nerve-centre that has brought together rival political leaders, and has \[generated\] a powerful social movement bent on bringing joy and hope to millions of underprivileged people in poverty-stricken KwaZulu Natal.”

It may even be considered that the Festival for the Children of the Rainbow Nation in 1997 is the movement’s single most significant event, in terms of social interaction, since the inception of ISKCON by its founder, Śrīla Prabhupāda, in 1966.

This, truly, is “a watershed in the history of the International Society for Krishna Consciousness.”

\* \* \*

## Bhaktivedanta College

Bhaktivedanta College

Out of thousands of universities all over the world, not one teaches the science of the soul, yet the soul is the most important part of the body. Śrīla Prabhupāda deplored the utter lack of spiritual education in western society and left behind clear guidelines for the implementation of degree courses and awards in spiritual knowledge, grounded in *śāstra*, the Vedic scriptures.

The Bhaktivedanta College of Education and Culture, putting into practice the desire of Śrīla Prab-hupāda, has had over 6,000 students to its credit.

Founded 1991 in Durban by Bṛhat-mṛdaṅga dāsa, the College, this “king of education,” is uniquely placed to initiate a renaissance in spiritual science, specifically the Gauḍīya-Vaiṣṇava philosophy of *bhakti-yoga* as propounded by Śrīla Prabhupāda, throughout the academic world.

This is quite feasible. As society becomes ever more desperate to learn to cope with the relentless toll of worldwide social upheaval and disorder—not to mention the cataclysmic natural disasters that fall thick and fast about our ears—its need for an alternative socio-philosophical system will fill the vacuum created by the pursuit of an exclusively physical science that has, for too long, denied the validity of its counterpart, metaphysical science.

The institution needed to fill the gap—Bhaktivedanta College—is already in place, and so is the dynamic element, namely Prabhupāda’s books, which constitute the most comprehensive compilation of spiritual science and philosophy ever put into print. Thus, by the mercy of Śrīla Prabhupāda, the *kalpa-vṛk*ṣa of Bhaktivedanta College will spread its branches ever wider, bearing the ripened fruit of Kṛṣṇa consciousness worldwide.

\* \* \*

The policies of the College are progressive and innovative. Whereas, in general, students have to go to a college, Bhaktivedanta College *goes out to the students!* This makes good sense because Kṛṣṇa consciousness is meant to expand more amongst the congregation than within the temples. Classes are held in halls, schools, private homes, ISKCON centres—anywhere in fact that is congenial—and are widely advertised through local media and public libraries, etc.

The tutorials, which are part-time (evening) and of short duration—one and a half hours, or three hours of a weekend for higher studies—are accessible to a broad spectrum of the public.

The qualifications offered are comprehensive, including two certificates for ages four to sixteen. For adults there is the Bhakti-sadācara Certificate, followed by the Bhakti-gītā Diploma and then the Bhakti-śāstri Degree. The degree is of equivalent standard to that of the establishment universities.

The College is unique in that it trains its own lecturers—mainly former students. They take the Vaiṣṇava

Training and Education Teacher’s Certificate and follow a strict observance of sixteen rounds *japa* and the four regulative principles as instructed by Śrīla Prabhupāda.

The College began in 1991 with just two lecturers, and forty-eight students who, after qualifying for the VTE Teacher’s Certificate, were encouraged to begin classes in their own areas. They in turn similarly encouraged their students. In this way the programme has expanded quite dramatically.

The Bhaktivedanta College of Education and Culture has spread rapidly, establishing centres in South Africa, and in Canada, Guyana, Holland, Hong Kong, India, Kenya, Mauritius, Mexico, the Philippines, Puerto Rico, Russia, Trinidad and the USA.

Bṛhat-mṛdaṅga dāsa had the vision and the necessary expertise to implement the desire of Śrīla Prabhupāda: To establish an institute for the systematic study of Vedic literature and philosophy as presented by His Divine Grace. A former teacher, he joined ISKCON in 1983 and lectured extensively, in both schools and universities, on the balance of spiritual and material education.

He further prepared himself through the Preacher Training Course at the Vṛndāvana Institute of Higher Education in India. On returning to South Africa, he launched Bhaktivedanta College in July 1991. Bṛhat-Mṛdaṅga has never since looked back. Some comments by ISKCON leaders:

Bhakti-tīrtha Swami, GBC: “The Bhaktivedanta College programme . . . *is the most sophisticated spiritual institution in the world and is creating a tremendous revolution in consciousness.*

“The structure of the programme and the type of devotees participating in teaching and administration is overwhelming. Bhaktivedanta College students and teachers will definitely be leaders in Kṛṣṇa conscious communities around the world.”

Romapada Swami, GBC: “My experience of Bhaktivedanta College has convinced me that the vision of congregational members training others in the essentials of Kṛṣṇa life can be done on a massscale. *The effectiveness of this particular programme vastly exceeded my expectations.”*

And Tamala Krishna Goswami, GBC, described the College programme as “an essential educational experience which is both intellectually stimulating and life enriching.”

Bhaktivedanta College is also enthusiastically supported in Mauritius by a prominent politician, the Hon. Kadress R.C. Pillay, Minister of Education and Human Resource Development.

The basic aim of the College is, simply, to expand. Navina Krishna dāsa, GBC, has pointed out that, “The BCEC is doing a service as important as book distribution.” The BBT, ISKCON’s long-established publishing institution, needs to balance its high-powered book distribution alongside supportive academic study courses. Working in harness, the BBT and Bhaktivedanta College may well conquer the world of Academe, but without the College the BBT may well remain, as Navina Krishna dāsa puts it, “a mere seller of books.”

Bhakti Caitanya Swami, a senior leader in South Africa and Chairman of the Board of Examiners for BCEC, wrote to the congregation: “Śrīla Prabhupāda wanted his devotees to read all his books. They are not simply there for selling to others. If you are trying to get someone to take one of Śrīla Prabhupāda’s books and they ask you what it is all about and you are not able to tell them, then it is not good. So you should all take very seriously the need to read all of Śrīla Prabhupāda’s books and become well-versed in the message contained in them. Kṛṣṇa consciousness is a science, and therefore needs to be seriously studied.”

If ISKCON is to enter the heart of society as a dynamic worldwide preaching organisation, its practitioners, the devotees, must needs be academically qualified to be a match for and to be taken seriously by this dizzily-spiralling high-tech computerised society.

This is where, by the grace of Śrīla Prabhupāda, the Bhaktivedanta College of Education and Culture comes into its own.

## Pres. de Klerk Congratulates ISKCON

Pres. de Klerk Congratulates ISKCON

In a statement welcoming the annual Festival of the Chariots, Mr F.W. de Klerk, Nobel prizewinner and President of South Africa, praised the devotees for their work in promoting peace and cultural harmony in South Africa.

On 18th November 1993, Indradyumna Swami was introduced to President de Klerk by Cllr Ashwin Mohanlall at Durban City Hall. The President was in Durban to meet up with the city’s chief cultural leaders and businessmen. Just hours before the meeting, the President and Nelson Mandela of the ANC had signed their historic agreement on a new South African constitution.

Indradyumna Swami first offered the President a fragrant flower garland. The President remarked, “The beautiful garland smells wonderful. It makes me feel wonderful and I know that it carries with it great meaning. Thank you very much.”

Presenting the President with a *Bhagavad-gītā As It Is*, Indradyumna Swami explained that this ancient scripture could help South Africa in its transitional period towards a new government. “Real peace and harmony will be achieved when all of us—black, white, coloured and Indian—realise what we have in common; that is, that we are all spiritual souls, eternal servants of God.”

The President said that he appreciated these remarks as he was also a firm believer in God. Indradyumna Swami briefly discussed with the President the role of leadership in the ancient Vedic culture—how the head of state ruled according to the laws of God as given in the revealed scriptures, such as *Bhagavad-gītā.*

During their exchange, Indradyumna Swami asked the President if he remembered visiting ISKCON’s centre in Chatsworth, the Temple of Understanding, ten years ago during its construction, when the President was Minister of Home Affairs. At the finish of their discussion, Indradyumna Swami presented him with a copy of *The Science of Self-Realisation* and a box of *prasādam* sweets.

President de Klerk later sent a message of congratulations to ISKCON that was read out at the festival. In a statement, President de Klerk said: “On behalf of the Government and people of South Africa, I take great pleasure in wishing the International Society for Krishna Consciousness the best of everything for this year’s Ratha-yātrā festival in Durban.

“Not only is the celebration of this ancient Festival of Chariots of particular spiritual and cultural value to the Hindu community of South Africa, but also it has become a major tourist attraction for the people of Durban, residents and visitors alike, over the Christmas holiday period.

“I hope that the festival this year will bring together thousands of people in peace and happiness, as it has done in the past, and thereby contribute to the building of a peaceful, reconciled and united nation, for which we are striving as we cross the threshold to the new and democratic South Africa this festive season.”

President de Klerk was awarded the Nobel Prize for Peace in 1993, jointly with Nelson Mandela, for dismantling apartheid and introducing the democratic process in South Africa.

## Mission Almost Impossible

Chapter 13  
Mission *Almost* Impossible

It was during the early nineties, while I was involved in the campaign to save Bhaktivedanta Manor Temple from closure, that a very worthwhile challenge presented itself. Quite by chance I came across a magazine called *India Link International* (published in the UK, May 1994), which had for its cover story Nelson Mandela’s early struggles. Inside was a lengthy feature on the threatened closure of our Hare Krishna temple, with pictures of 35,000 massed protesters marching in London in support of the temple. One of the pictures revealed my daughter, Viṣṇu-priyā dāsī, right at the front of the procession. On the opposite page, Nelson Mandela on his first trip to India was pictured offering *praṇāma*.

Believing this to be a meaningful coincidence, I derived great inspiration from it. There and then I decided that it was my duty to try and arrange a meeting with the President. I did realise, however, that I was about to commit myself to a seemingly impossible mission. Getting to meet Nelson Mandela is like “shooting the rhino!” This was an expression used by Śrīla Prabhupāda; it means attempting something which is practically impossible. If you succeed, people are amazed; if you do not, they will say, “Well, it couldn’t be done, anyway.”

I consulted with Śrutidharma, the head priest and fellow campaigner at Bhaktivedanta Manor Temple, who so nicely gave encouragement, telling me in a very sincere way, “Riddha, go for it! Make it your priority to meet Nelson Mandela.” Greatly reassured I took it to heart.

In November 1994 I had just missed my opportunity when Nelson Mandela visited our Temple of Understanding in South Africa. Maybe Lord Śrī Kṛṣṇa simply did not want it to happen at that time. I then heard from one of my fellow devotee-countrymen that Nelson Mandela was definitely scheduled to be guest of honour of the Government of India for the Republic Day festival in New Delhi, in January 1995.

So I thought, “Wonderful! This is my opportunity to connect.” It was then that I approached Prāṇabandu dāsa and he also liked the idea. He helped me raise some funds to get things moving. I also scraped together some of my own money for the remaining expenses that would be incurred for the tickets, etc.

As soon as the plans were finalised, I sat down and in a moment of desperation, I broke out in a sweat. I actually began to shiver with uncertainty, because I suddenly realised, “What have I got myself involved in? Now I’ve made a promise to the devotees to deliver the goods.” I asked myself, “How am I going to accomplish this? But I‘ve got to go through with it—it’s too late to turn back now.”

I knew that my daughter, Viṣṇu-priyā, although only fourteen, would be a great inspiration and a good companion. Featured in the *India Link International* magazine and proactive in the Bhaktivedanta Manor Temple campaign, she was a prominent campaigner for the threatened temple. She and others had handed a strongly-worded protest to the Mayor of the local council, entitled the Children’s Charter for Freedom of Religion.

Feeling strong in my conviction, I bought the airline tickets and a visa all in one day. I had to move fast now as Nelson Mandela had already arrived in India and would be there for a short time only. The night before I was due to leave, I invited over a close spiritually-minded friend of mine, Steve, and his good wife Laarni, for some *prasādam*. That evening I told them of my plan to fly to India with my daughter to petition President Mandela to help us in our mission.

I shall never forget that wonderful moment when my dear friends helped us enter into a deep level of prayer. We called on the holy name of Lord Śrī Kṛṣṇa, and I asked for the blessings of Śrīla Prabhupāda and his confidential servitors to prevail over all adversity and bring success to our mission. As we prayed, I was overcome by a sense of elation that was to sustain us in the days ahead. From that point on, I felt that our footsteps were being guided by a spiritual presence, and that we were about to embark on a mystical journey. Afterwards we departed for the airport and with renewed confidence we flew to New Delhi. Our mystical journey had begun.

Arriving in India was like stepping into an ancient epoch where time stood still. But the reality was that we were a full day behind Mandela’s schedule. My main contact, a South African businessman, had given me the wrong address in New Delhi, and I was at a loss regarding what to do.

How would we even begin? The three days left of Nelson Mandela’s visit were to be spent in high profile with India’s President and Prime Minister. Impossible as a meeting with him appeared to be, there was still a slim chance on the last day, in spite of our late arrival.

Viṣṇu-priyā was so tired from travelling that I found her a guest-room and she went straight to bed. I locked the door and went out to the local temple’s Centennial office nearby to get some peace of mind and find my bearings. I walked there in an uncertain mood, somewhat distraught.

It was about nine or ten at night when I met Lokanātha Swami. He had been having a Prabhupāda Centennial meeting. I talked with him and told him of my plans and of how I had lost my contact, which was making things even more difficult. We then began discussing the Prabhupāda Centennial and that got me really inspired. Just by meeting him my hopes were renewed and my flagging spirits raised—confirmation that I was indeed on the right track.

After I had spent some time with Lokanātha Swami, I sat down in the little office by myself, feeling my confidence growing. I telephoned the South African High Commission, but it was closed. However, I did speak to a security officer, explaining that I was a colleague of one of the South African delegates and that I was trying to contact my friend. He gave me a telephone number which turned out to be the residence of the State President of India! When I called and explained that I was originally from South Africa—which was true—they gave me my contact’s hotel address and telephone number. So I hit the jackpot! I got my break.

At about midnight I asked a devotee to give me a lift on the back of his scooter and I went round to the hotel where my contact was staying, hoping to see him, but he was not there. I waited patiently and thirty minutes later he walked in, having attended a function with President Mandela.

He was with two other delegates whom I had not met before, and when I introduced myself he was a bit distant. I gave him the most wonderful photograph of the presiding Deities of our temple, Śrī Śrī Rādhā-Gokulānanda, in Their *Rādhāṣṭhamī* outfit. Being the Kṛṣṇa *bhakta* that he was, he regarded the photograph intently, and then in a second his heart melted. I explained to him how I had co-ordinated Śrīla Prabhupāda’s visit to South Africa in 1975, and gave him an update on the campaign. I urged him to help me and my daughter to arrange a meeting with President Mandela.

One of his colleagues interrupted, saying, “We can’t do that. It would embarrass the President, and that would embarrass the Indian Government.”

So I said, “Well, if you can’t help us to meet him, then at least allow my daughter to garland him.”

I showed him a little photograph of my daughter and he said, “I’ll agree to do that.” So we agreed that I would meet the delegates in the hotel lobby at nine o’clock the next morning and they would take my daughter to see President Mandela.

Next, I had to get some garlands. First thing in the morning, I ventured out to the Hanumān temple, which was not too far from the hotel. Sure enough, there were people there already, selling garlands at seven o’clock in the morning. Mr Ramnarayan, the more favourable delegate, had given us several hundred rupees in expenses, which were used to purchase beautiful garlands, thick with flowers, including a very expensive crimson rose garland for Mr Mandela.

After the garlands were made, I requested them to be offered in the temple right then and there to Śrī Hanumānjī. I had a shoe *wallah* shine Viṣṇu-priyā’s shoes spotlessly. Dressed in a beautiful silk *sari* and wearing fresh *tilaka*, she looked first-rate.

Accompanied by ISKCON’s director of communications, my friend Mahāmantra dāsa, we left for the delegates’ hotel. Mahāmantra was doubtful and said, “No, you can’t greet Mandela with a garland. You will not be allowed to give him a garland. Don’t take it. I’ve already spoken to the Indian security and it will be forbidden.”

But I said, “ No! We do this to honour him. We must do it. I will not give up!”

So he said, “Fair enough, Riddha, you try, but I don’t think you’ll be allowed in.”

We waited in the lobby of the hotel and as they came down my daughter gave the garlands to the delegates. They were so happy. The same people who had discouraged me so strongly the previous night were now positively glowing with enthusiasm.

But suddenly they changed and became very reluctant again, saying, “We can’t go to President Mandela wearing garlands and then not offer him one.” Finally I convinced them that at least Mr Ramnarayan should wear a garland. It was agreed.

We all got into two chauffeur-driven white Ambassador cars and drove to the presidential palace, with Mr Ramnarayan leading the way. He sat in the front seat with his fresh garland looking very much the VIP. Viṣṇu-priyā and I were in the back seat, looking very “official” in the motorcade *en route* to the Indian presidential palace.

We went through numerous security barriers before getting anywhere near the palace. When questioned, it was pointed out that Viṣṇu-priyā was accompanying Mr Ramnarayan, and we also got in as guests. Fortunately, Mahāmantra prabhu was well known on the Indian circuit, having attended many official functions and being a former High Court attorney. He dealt with the security people, which eased the situation.

On arrival in the forecourt of the palace grounds, further stricter security checks began. There were many sniffer-dogs and what seemed like sixty or more security men spread throughout the palace with walkie-talkies. They started to check us more rigorously.

Mr Ramnarayan had second thoughts and said, “Let Viṣṇu-priyā stay outside. I’ll go inside myself, first.” He more or less backed off.

Thinking that Viṣṇu-priyā was not going to get in, I stood my ground and said, “No. Mr Ramnarayan, you take her—in she goes!” I had faith in Kṛṣṇa that we should push ahead.

In the knick of time, a friend of mine, Amichand Rajabansi, suddenly turned up. Known as “the Tiger,” he was a famous Indian South African MP. Mr Rajabansi, a devotee of Śrī Hanumānjī, just took my daughter in without any fuss through the one remaining door. At last, she was in. I stayed outside with Mahāmantra prabhu. Once inside she was questioned and searched a further two times.

Mr Ramnarayan, more confident now, simply said, “She’s here with us to give a garland to the President.” And the security personnel agreed. That was it. I watched the comings and goings from an observation area.

About fifteen minutes later, I saw the car carrying the President of India enter, followed by an ambulance. He invariably has an ambulance with him in case of an attack. Then a decoy car with a look-alike President Mandela arrived. Finally, President Mandela himself was driven in, followed by dignitaries from many important countries. There was a lot of commotion as he entered.

Once inside, however, in front of numerous foreign dignitaries and diplomats, Viṣṇu-priyā was personally introduced to President Mandela. She garlanded him, and then they stepped to one side for a short informal talk.

She declared to him that she was representing one million Hindus in the United Kingdom. She told him, “I hear that you are a champion of human rights and I have come with a special plea for you to help save our temple.” Viṣṇu-priyā explained in her own way why she had come and asked him to help us.

Mr Mandela acknowledged her courageousness and inquired what career she was going to enter. In reply she said that she wanted to be a civil rights lawyer. Mandela said that he was also once a lawyer and as a result he ended up in jail for most of his life! They shared an innocent laugh at that. She received a kiss of thanks on her cheek from Mandela as stunned bystanders looked on. The burning question on everyone’s lips was: “Who is that girl?”

Their talk over, Mr Mandela sat down with some high-powered South African businessmen for a discussion. Viṣṇu-priyā stood to one side and talked to Mandela’s daughter. Then, noticing that she was standing, President Mandela requested for her to come over and be seated alongside him. “She can come and sit with us, too,” he said. She sat down next to Mr Mandela. Some top South African Indian businessmen together with the de Beers of the diamond mining industry were also at the meeting.

Before the meeting ended, Mr Ramnarayan began to explain about the religious persecution at Bhaktivedanta Manor. The President replied that as he was leaving shortly for South Africa he would see our envoy there to discuss the matter privately, at his leisure.

As the discussion came to an end, the President told Viṣṇu-priyā to give his personal regards to all our followers back home at the temple. I heard afterwards that Viṣṇu-priyā was very calm and precise. Mr Ramnarayan’s colleagues from Durban were very impressed. Afterwards, I joined the South African delegates when they went outside to be photographed.

I was highly elated. It was all like a dream—and our prayers had been answered. “Mission *almost* impossible” had been accomplished.

\* \* \*

As a result of the successful meeting in India, there was a full-page colour coverage in the South African newspaper, *Sunday Tribune.* Our international campaigning appeared to be on a roller-coaster.

Viṣṇu-priyā and I continued to campaign for justice by bringing international pressure to bear in South Africa. This was highlighted when, on a state visit to South Africa in March 1995, HM the Queen was informed on at least two occasions of the controversy concerning religious intolerance in the UK.

The protest was spearheaded by Bhaktin Lilly (now Pārsada dāsī) of the ISKCON Durban temple. When a mass demonstration against British bigotry and religious injustice organised to take place during the Queen’s visit to Durban was cancelled, Bhaktin Lilly seized the opportunity to petition the Queen in person. The encounter was highlighted when her meeting with the Queen was featured on the front page of *The Leader.* (See appendix: “Krishna lass slips note to the Queen.”)

This was followed up by her uncle, Mr Ranjith Ramnarain, a prominent businessman, who had tea with the Queen at Durban City Hall. Mr Ramnarain brought the British authorities’ threatened closure of Bhaktivedanta Manor, the sister temple of the Temple of Understanding in Durban, to the attention of the Queen.

Further progress was made at the World Hindu Conference in Durban in July 1995, when chairman Mr Ramnarain set the scene for our vice-chairman, Cllr Frank Ward (Arjuna dāsa) of the Hare Krishna Temple Defence Movement (HKTDM), to exclusively inform President Nelson Mandela of the threat of closure facing Bhaktivedanta Manor.

Cllr Frank Ward explained to President Mandela about the failure of the United Kingdom to have a written constitution with a Bill of Rights to protect freedom of religious worship and practice. President Mandela was puzzled by the possibility of a British government closing any place of worship. A few months later (6th November), the temple was fined £37,000 for permitting public worship.

The following year on May 10th, after a massive campaign of protest and civil disobedience waged by the British and South African devotees and supporters, a remarkable announcement was made by John Gummer, the Secretary of State for Environment: “Bhaktivedanta Manor granted permission for public worship.”

On 9th July 1996, President Mandela arrived in London, and that day three High Court judges quashed the original fine of £37,000, reducing it to £5,000. By coincidence, my daughter Viṣṇu-priyā got to see President Mandela’s private secretary and, on behalf of Bhaktivedanta Manor, presented a traditional flower garland for the President.

This unique assistance from the former oppressed country of South Africa demonstrated a sympathetic understanding towards all who are subjected to bigotry and religious intolerance, and even hatred, in the so-called mother of democracy, Great Britain. Indeed, the handful of South African devotees that helped Bhaktivedanta Manor in its darkest hour shall never be forgotten.

## Śrīla Prabhupāda’s Centennial

Chapter 14  
Śrīla Prabhupāda’s Centennial

Prabhupāda’s date of birth, 1st September 1896, was very auspicious. Indeed, his horoscope confirmed the prediction of previous *ācāryas* that Abhay (as he was named at birth), by the age of seventy, would traverse the world as an exalted, renounced teacher, establishing 108 temples worldwide. He was in fact later to become known as His Divine Grace A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami Prabhupāda.

In 1996, ISKCON held celebrations worldwide for Prabhupāda’s Centennial. In the year before, the Temple of Understanding had already begun celebrations with the commemoration of the twentieth anniversary of Śrīla Prabhupāda’s visit to Durban City Hall, in October 1975.

I was privileged to be invited to attend this auspicious function. As one of the first to help establish ISKCON in South Africa, I was impressed by the evident mood of devotion and by the degree of respect shown, even before the Centennial. The whole event was inspired by local devotees and organised by Śruta Kirtī dāsa, the temple president of Chatsworth, a disciple of Bhakti-caru Swami.

Arriving in Durban from London just one hour before the programme began, I proceeded directly to the venue. On entering the foyer in Durban City Hall, I was overwhelmed by the spiritual atmosphere created by the occasion of Prabhupāda’s Centennial.

In the foyer there was a photographic exhibition of ISKCON South Africa and Śrīla Prabhupāda’s visit to Durban. I was impressed. As I entered the hall, it began to fill up with admirers of Śrīla Prabhupāda. This was the programme bill:

**DURBAN CITY HALL**

**Tuesday 10 October 1995**

PROGRAMME

6.00 p.m. Opening of Photo Exhibition

Śrīla Prabhupāda in South Africa

ISKCON 1975 to 1995

Śrīla Prabhupāda Centennial 1996

7.00 p.m. Bhajans by the Hare Krishna Children

Arrival of Śrīla Prabhupāda *mūrti*

Appreciation by Guests of Honour

Cultural Dances

Offering of Lamps

20th Anniversary Cake Cutting

Dedication to Śrīla Prabhupāda—

Hare Krishna Band

Address by ISKCON Governing Body

Commissioner, His Holiness Bhakti-caru

Swami : “He Built a House in which the

Whole World can Live”

Bhajans and Kīrtans

9.30 p.m. Vegetarian Dinner

At 7.00 p.m. Syāmlāl dāsa and I carried Prabhupāda’s life-size *mūrti* in through the same entrance that His Divine Grace had used twenty years before, and placed it centre-stage overlooking the celebrations.

Once the programme got under way, Lokabandu, the programme co-ordinator, asked me to address the hundreds of people in the packed hall. Having already compiled many chapters of my book, *Destination South Africa*, I took the opportunity to talk about some of my recollections to the audience. After the address, I offered my unfinished manuscript to the *mūrti* of Śrīla Prabhupāda and asked for blessings for its satisfactory completion. While writing I had gone to great lengths to research the subject matter. In my heart I felt a great respect for those devotees who had come before me to South Africa, and who had risked their youth and their very lives in order to preach on behalf of Śrīla Prabhupāda.

One such person was Kṣudhi dāsa. In a mood of re-uniting Śrīla Prabhupāda’s family, I arranged, with the help of Śruta Kirtī dāsa the temple president, to have Kṣudhi dāsa brought over from America to attend the celebrations. It was a great pleasure to have Kṣudhi back after his untimely departure in 1972. Kṣudhi was also asked to address the audience, and shared his memories of the early days with us.

Afterwards Indradyumna Swami spoke. The evening was given a cultural flavour, with first-class Bharat Natyam and Kathak dance, and music by Boy George—“Bow Down Mister,” which worked out well. When the song “Bow Down Mister” was first released, it was an immediate hit, staying at number one in the South African charts for many months.

Next to address the audience was Mr P.V. Lakani, President of the South African Hindu Maha Sabha, followed by Mr Anil Anand, Indian Consul (Culture and Information). After some South Indian dance there was an address by the chairperson of ISKCON KwaZulu Natal, Lokabandhu dāsa. A special guest was Dr Z.L. Mkhize, Natal Minister of Health, who seemed quite overwhelmed by the evening’s mood of devotion.

The main address—the highlight of the evening—was given by His Holiness Bhakti-caru Swami, ISKCON’s Minister of Culture. His talk was followed by a presentation of the twentieth anniversary cake, which was offered to Śrīla Prabhupāda with numerous flags of the “United Nations of the spiritual world.”

Rounding off the evening very nicely, His Holiness Bhakti-caru Swami and his disciples sang soothing *bhajanas*. Śruta Kirtī dāsa prabhu gave a vote of thanks, and I was impressed by the way that the whole programme was managed in a very devotional mood. Numerous other Centennial offerings were made to Śrīla Prabhupāda in the months ahead, especially in 1996.

One special event to honour Śrīla Prabhupāda in his Centennial was the naming of “Bhaktivedanta Swami Circle.” For some time the Chatsworth temple had been in dispute with the local civic authority over their desire to change the name of the road leading to the temple. The struggle continued for months on end. Finally, the change of name was achieved and out of respect for ISKCON’s founder, it was named Bhaktivedanta Swami Circle. This was, indeed, a milestone and a good example for ISKCON temples around the world to follow, because the founder *ācārya’s* name should always come first.

## Forging Ahead

Chapter 15  
Forging Ahead

Over the past few years, I have had the pleasure of associating with and discussing the subject matter of this book and the future of South Africa with His Holiness Bhakti-tīrtha Swami. He has for many years been one of the more prominent leaders of the International Society for Krishna Consciousness, both in South Africa and the rest of the world. A frequent visitor to South Africa, he has been interviewed numerous times on national television and radio there.

Bhakti-tīrtha Swami took part in a short but successful programme on national television in prime-time, called “Good Morning, South Africa.” This took place when Bill Clinton, the American president, was on a state visit to South Africa.

In 1995 Bhakti-tīrtha Swami was featured on the controversial Radio *702* phone-in talk show in Durban. He was such a success that his phone-in went on from 7pm to 10pm. The programme was called “Believe it or Not.” It was broadcast nationwide and the hostess was Kate Tarkington. He has been a regular contributor to the talk show ever since.

In a discussion with her he had this to say: “In many ways South Africa is a mirror for the present civilisation, both worldwide and in ISKCON. The international community has continuously observed South Africa as it struggled with issues of racism, tribalism, elitism, sexism and authoritarianism.

“Former President Nelson Mandela still enjoys love and respect from around the world. We pray that his unifying actions will help. Apartheid has been dismantled, but this has only helped superficially in some areas. In many areas, illicit activities such as prostitution, drug abuse, theft, child abuse and homicide are rampant. As a matter of fact, Johannesburg has become the homicide capital of the world.

“In this very troubled time, we offer our hand to uplift South Africans into Kṛṣṇa consciousness. Śrīla Prabhupāda and his followers established ISKCON under the most difficult and dangerous circumstances. Not only is the Durban Hare Kṛṣṇa temple one of the most beautiful temples in the world, but also the Durban festivals, educational programmes, Food for Life and *nāma haṭṭas* have excelled among devotee communities. Nowhere in the world have so many national leaders come to associate and attend our functions as in South Africa.

“But in one sense this is somewhat superficial. Sure, ISKCON has come as far as the South African Government has with the new regime, but in both cases there is so much potential that has yet to be properly or fully utilised. The country is so much in need of a real sense of unity-with-diversity based on a healthy community spirit. This can only come about when the people, and especially the genuine leaders, are able to develop a truly competent, principle-centred, God-conscious society.

“Now the task is at hand. This is, of course, ultimately what Śrīla Prabhupāda wanted and is still waiting for.”

In conclusion Bhakti-tīrtha Swami said, “The material factor of South Africa is extremely precarious, but with proper spiritual guidance—from ISKCON—South Africa can still be saved. Devotees can show what is possible through the sacred transcendental Vaiṣṇava teachings, under the guidance of such an exalted *ācārya* as His Divine Grace A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami Prabhupāda.”

In the past decade, Bhakti-tīrtha Swami has been representing ISKCON in an exemplary way. He has been continually sought after by the press for unprecedented news coverage, in particular the *Sunday Tribune* with a full-page article in 1994.

At the World Hindu Conference in 1995 (July 7-10) at the University of Durban-Westville, I attended several lectures. Of particular interest was Bhakti-tīrtha Swami’s hour-long lecture, which was well attended by Hindu delegates from all over the world—businessmen, academics and politicians.

This is a short excerpt from his lecture:

The world has a lacking of spiritual understanding of *śāstra, sādhu, guru.* In reflection we should look to Bhārata and its *ācāryas* such as Buddha, Mādhava, Caitanya Mahāprabhu . . . India is the *guru* of the world; it has the ability to export the Vedic knowledge and wisdom to solve many of the problems of the world. ISKCON wants people to be aware of spiritual technologies.

What has gone wrong in South Africa is something we find in other parts of the global community. One of the biggest problems is the intense investment and absorption in materialism. Absorption in materialism as a priority creates greed and exploitation . . . Our *sanātana-dharma* is able to have real unity-with-diversity. Access to this knowledge is trans-global. That is the knowledge of the Vedic paradigm . . . Many heads of states are being advised by *svāmīs* and sages, \[since\] heads of states are accountable for 32 per cent of national *karma* of a country.

A disciple of His Divine Grace A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami Prabhupāda, Bhakti-tīrtha Swami, the only Afro-American *guru* within ISKCON, is a much revered *sannyāsī.*

His much-applauded speech at the World Hindu Conference rally on 9th July 1995 was very much appreciated. Bhakti-tīrtha Swami addressed an audience of some 35,000. The highlight of the occasion was when he had them all chanting out aloud the Hare Kṛṣṇa *mantra*.

Hare Kṛṣṇa, Hare Kṛṣṇa, Kṛṣṇa Kṛṣṇa, Hare Hare  
Hare Rāma, Hare Rāma, Rāma Rāma, Hare Hare

He has a special rapport and affinity with other religious organisations and cultural groups, and has exemplary dealings with government leaders. Nelson Mandela received a copy of Bhakti-tīrtha Swami’s book *Leadership for an Age of Higher Consciousness*, when they were together at the Festival for the Children of the Rainbow Nation in Durban on 23rd April 1997. Pārsada dāsī of Food for Life also presented a copy of the very same book to Mr Thabo Mbeki at the Presidential Job Summit on 17th September 1998.

In the October 1999 edition of the South African Broadcasting Corporation’s staff journal *News Magazine*, Bhakti-tīrtha Swami was pictured on the front cover with SABC’s chief executive, the Revd Hawa Mbatha.

The Revd Mbatha had committed the Corporation to supporting the Food for Life project and the “Stop Raping our Children” campaign, saying that the corporation must co-operate with other organisations that share the same objectives in order to be effective. His Holiness Bhakti-tīrtha Swami was present, together with Revd Mbatha, at the launch of the two ventures, which were co-ordinated by Pārsada dāsī in conjunction with SABC. The Revd Mbatha said:

It is in this spirit \[of co-operation\] that we accept any type of partnership which is aimed at the challenges of child abuse, abuse of women, aids, morality, crime and all the other ills that are plaguing our society today.

Speaking at the launch, Bhakti-tīrtha Swami said that the media is today overwhelmed with messages that promote lust and not love. He said that in Africa today, almost forty-five per cent of the population are children below the age of fifteen, and as the world increasingly becomes a very violent place, it is the children who suffer.

Seminars specifically designed to meet the needs of senior South African government officials in the field of peace and conflict resolution are being proposed. The director of an international body, the Centre of Conflict Resolution based in South Africa, has requested Bhakti-tīrtha Swami to promote a socio-political model of conflict resolution in order to take participants through a process of consciousness-raising and spiritual transformation. Bhakti-tīrtha, a graduate of Princeton University who majored in psychology, is a master of *bhakti-yoga* (spiritual science) and has received accolades in many other fields, including African studies, Indology, politics and international law. He is thus well qualified for this remarkable undertaking. These workshops are expected to take place at the University of Cape Town, where the Centre has its facilities.

Although there are immense problems besetting South African society in the aftermath of the dismantling of the apartheid regime, there is considerable motivation for radical reform amongst many of its leaders. And they are now aware of and enlivened by the great reforming zeal of ISKCON devotees, such as His Holiness Bhakti-tīrtha Swami, a spiritual warrior who is without material or political motivation and practising high religious principles.

Kṛṣṇa consciousness is not dependent upon any type of politics: that was the verdict of Śrīla Prabhupāda’s spiritual master. And so it is that humanity cannot afford to wait for political “solutions.” They are at best mere stopgaps.

When Prabhupāda was asked for his views on South Africa, he immediately broadened the issue, saying that all over the world civilization is being misdirected. His conclusion was that a society without spiritual knowledge can never make peace formulas. Reliance on elaborate socio-political systems and “defence” measures geared to high technology is ultimately futile because, as Prabhupāda’s title “Bhaktivedanta” indicates, “the end of all knowledge is to engage in devotional service \[to God\].”

That is why the ISKCON movement is dedicated to the dissemination of *bhakti-yoga* throughout the whole world. Kṛṣṇa consciousness, devotional service to God, is the original peace formula as authenticated by all the great *ācāryas* of India, such as Śrī Caitanya Mahāprabhu and His confidential associates, from time immemorial.

## The Mystique of Krishna Consciousness

The Mystique of Krishna Consciousness

Krishna Sect Leader Pays Visit to SA

***Story by LambertPringle***

***Religion Reporter***

***Pictures by John Pauling***

With the mystique of one of the world’s oldest cultures behind his eyes, the leader of the Hare Krishna movement has paid his first visit to South Africa.

With their shaven heads, painted faces and flowing robes, Hare Krishna devotees have become a common sight in Johannesburg’s streets.

Now, His Divine Grace, A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami Prabhupada is here to explain the mysteries of Krishna consciousness.

At the Hare Krishna temple in Muller Street, Yeoville, Johannesburg, Srila Prabhupada said his mission was to help people understand the science of God.

Although he is 80 years old, he travels the world.

His message is one of peace and love.

He has established more than 80 centres of Krishna consciousness around the world—two of them in Johannesburg and Durban.

He will also make his first public appearance in Johannesburg tonight in the Great Hall at the University of Witwatersrand.

**Yellow Clay**

His forehead and nose painted with yellow clay from ancient rivers in India, he has also written and translated more than 50 books. His best-known work is the *‘Bhagavad-gita As It Is.’*

In an interview he explained that to speak of Krishna was to speak of God.

He says that an animal cannot know what God is, but a human being can . . . “Nature gives us this human body to understand God.

“Unless we co-operate with the Supreme Lord, our life is baffled.

**No value**

“A screw that has fallen from a machine has no value. But when the same screw is again attached to the machine it has value.

“In the same way we are part and parcel of God. Without God what is our value? No value!

“Similarly, if you always keep yourself spiritually engaged your body will act spiritually, although it is material.

“When an iron is hot, touch it anywhere and it will burn. It takes on the quality of fire. If you keep yourself in a state of Krishna consciousness, then you will become spiritualised. You will act spiritually and not have any more material demands,” he says.

**Six urges**

He warns that people are generally disturbed by six kinds of urges—the urge to talk, to become angry, and the urge of the mind, stomach, tongue and genitals. Through these urges material nature forces people to remain in the material world. It is only when one can overcome these urges that one experiences the spiritual purification of Krishna consciousness.

**Save Our temple plea to Mandela in India**

The daughter of a former South African mixed couple travelled thousands of kilometres from London to India to enlist the aid of President Mandela in preventing the closure of one of the largest Hindu temples in Britain.

**Yasanta Naidoo reports.**

Sari-clad London teenager Visnupriya was overjoyed the moment she set eyes on President Mandela in New Delhi, for she knew her 800 kilometre journey to meet him had not been in vain.

She placed a garland of crimson roses around President Mandela and pleaded for his help on behalf of one million Hindus in Britain. Visnupriya, 13, the daughter of former South African couple Roger May, formerly of Berea, Durban, and Benoni- born Nadira, who emigrated to London 23 years ago, made a special visit India to meet President Mandela and request his help in saving Bhaktivedanta Manor, a Hare Krishna temple situated outside London, from closure.

For the last 10 years Bhaktivedanta Manor, which was donated to the Hare Krishna movement by Beatle George Harrison, has been fighting an order to close issued by the Hertsmere Town Council because nearby residents complained of traffic congestion.

The town council banned public worship at the temple last year. Last month, the council prosecuted the temple president for allowing public worship at the temple during a major Hindu festival. The maximum fine for the contravention is R100 000.

Members of the temple submitted proposals to buy land at their own cost and build an access road to alleviate the traffic congestion. The proposal is currently being investigated through a public inquiry conducted by the council.

If the public inquiry rejects the proposals the temple will be closed. Days before President Mandela’s scheduled visit to India, Visnu Priya approached her parents, who left South Africa at the height of the apartheid era nearly two decades ago to escape the persecution they suffered as a mixed couple, with the idea of enlisting the aid of President Mandela in saving the temple.

Mr May, who is known as Riddha das, the name he adopted after \[joining\] the Hare Krishna movement in South Africa where he met his wife 23 years ago, said despite numerous bureaucratic problems such as having to organise passports and visas, his daughter was able to meet the president.

South African businessman Ranjith Ramnarain, who is a Hare Krishna devotee, had helped arrange for Visnu Priya to meet the president, Riddha das said.

“I was excited about meeting the president, but I was very calm. I told the president that I was addressing him on behalf of one million Hindus from the United Kingdom who were concerned and anxious about the temple’s fate,” Visnu Priya said.

“President Mandela told me he would look into the matter upon his return to SA. After my meeting, I sat in during a discussion between the president and SA Indian businessmen. It was am honour to meet the president and I am confident he will help save our temple.”

Riddha das claims the complaints by residents and action by the town council is based on racial and religious prejudices.

“It is evident from the council’s argument presented at the inquiry that they are concerned about Indian and Hindu worshippers in the village.

“Their council’s lawyer said in her argument that the high birth rate of children in Hindu families will drastically increase the number of worshippers,” Riddha das said.

He said it was “absurd in this day and age” to encounter such “racist attitudes by some members of the council” in modern, civilised society.

Frank Ward, Hertsmere Borough councillor, testified during the public inquiry this week that he recalled racist comments—such as “If I had my own way I would put the whole bloody lot on the plane back to Bombay”—made by some councillors about the Hindu worshippers at the temple.

He said he felt “deeply committed in my support for the members of the Hindu community against this alleged abuse of authority by Hertsmere.”

## Leader Voice Of The People

Leader Voice of the People

Krishna lass slips note to Queen

**By Juggie Naran**

“It was the work of a higher power. Lord Krishna works in mysterious ways.”

This is how a Chatsworth Hare Krishna devotee described her “incredible” meeting with Queen Elizabeth during her visit to Durban. Not only was she able to garland the monarch, she was also able to “slip” a letter of protest against efforts to close a Hindu temple in Britain.

“The miracle” meeting with the Queen and Bhaktin Lilly, public relations officer for the International Society for Krishna Consciousness, took place at the Greyville Race Course on Saturday.

“Not for a second did I believe that I would be given a chance to meet the Queen personally,” said Bhaktin Lilly. “However, I took along a garland and the letter in the hope that the incredible would happen. And it did.

“The PRO for Durban Unlimited, organisers of the event, asked me if I would like to garland the Queen. He then made the necessary arrangements. “I was then taken by the Queen’s security guard to meet her. There was a problem in putting the garland around her neck because of the wide brim of her hat. She asked to have the garland put around her right arm and this was when I managed to slip the letter to her.

“ I know that this is not protocol but what else could I do in the circumstances. This opportunity was granted by higher arrangement,” said Bhaktin Lilly.

This week *The Leader* can reveal the contents of the letter which shows concern by South African devotees over plans by certain British authorities to close down the Bhaktivedanta Manor temple in Letchmore Heath.

The letter also reveals proposals by local Hindu businessmen to cut economic ties with Britain as a mark of protest against the possible closure of the temple.

**CAMPAIGN**

Plans to close the temple led to a worldwide campaign by the British Hare Krishna movement to get groups from other countries to bring pressure on the British Government to halt the closure of the temple. There were fears in South Africa that local Hindu groups would stage a protest while the Queen was visiting the country.

But this was denied by ISKCON which had no such plans. The letter handed to the Queen said: “It is with great anxiety and trepidation I write this letter to you on behalf of the devotees of the International Society for Krishna Consciousness in South Africa.

“As you are well aware of, our sister temple, the Bhaktivedanta Manor in Letchmore Heath, is facing imminent closure due to bigotry and religious intolerance. South Africa has a long relationship with the United Kingdom and we look to Britain for its lead in democracy, equality and justice. Now our country has been relieved of the oppression of apartheid we have new opportunities for freedom of living, of business and culture. However, we are deeply shocked to learn of the treatment of Hindus in your country.

“The most important Hindu group in Britain has been fighting a very expensive legal battle to retain its rights to serve our community. The decision will be made early this year.

**INJUSTICE**

“The Hindu community of South Africa is totally against this grave injustice done to their brothers and sisters in your country.

“Letters of support have been sent to Prime Minister John Major from different sectors of the community including our dear friends from Umlazi who have signed petitions in favour of this abuse to religious freedom.

“Our President Mandela has given an assurance that he would look into this highly contentious issue.

“There has been talk among Hindu businessmen, who are a powerful backbone of the South African economy, of ignoring Britain in favour of other countries in their trade and business dealings.

“I beg you, as the Mother of Democracy, to dig deeply into your conscience and assist the Hindu community of Britain in not closing this place of worship.”

## Invitation

Invitation

Dear Reader

You are invited to visit any of our ISKCON centres worldwide if you have the opportunity to do so, and are especially invited to visit the Temple of Understanding in Chatsworth near Durban.

You may want to come and take time out on one of our many farm communities around the world and experience an alternative lifestyle of simple living and high thinking, see the temple-rooms and beautiful shrines, unwind in the peaceful gardens, walk around the extensive grounds, or witness the unique Cow Protection Programme, where our dairy herds of cows, calves and working oxen are forever safe from the horrors of the slaughterhouse.

Whether you want to discover the simple techniques of *japa* meditation or explore the depths of Vaiṣṇava philosophy, you will always find a devotee on hand to help you with your inquiries and answer your questions. You are welcome at any time. The following features you may find of special interest:

ancient ceremonies / chanting *japa*

questions and answers / lectures

At festivals, a full day of dramas and films followed by a free, sumptuous vegetarian feast. Check in the listing of ISKCON addresses that follow to enquire about:

Open days / Workshops

Cultural Studies / Festivals

Besides this you can experience temple worship, hear philosophical discourses and sample first-class cuisine in our vegetarian restaurants, or obtain our books, tapes, CDs and paraphernalia. For further information write or telephone to receive leaflets and route maps, dates or overnight accommodation. We look forward to hearing from you.

Hare Krishna!

Riddha Dāsa Adhikārī

***ISKCON Africa Mission***

ISKCON Africa Mission is a missionary programme that has been establishing preaching centres in Dar es Salaam, Tanzania and further afield. On a yearly basis ISKCON missionaries travel overland through Tanzania, Uganda, Malawi, Zambia, and Zimbabwe putting on festivals with dramas and films and distributing massive amounts of free, sumptuous *prasādam*. These festivals are normally held in the open, often in local stadiums. His Grace Tribhuvanatha dāsa Brahmācārya began these festivals which are now being continued by his supporters from Ireland and the UK. The travelling road show has ben going for many years. For future dates and locations of these festivals contact:

## Mis: About the Author

About the Author

Riddha Dāsa (Roger May), born 1949 in Durban, South Africa, left the country with his parents at the age of four to settle in England in 1954. They made the decision to leave their homeland because of the intolerant apartheid regime of the day.

He was educated in London, excelling in the arts and drama. In 1968 aged 19 he sailed around the world in the British merchant navy. The year after, he travelled overland throughout Europe and on to India, Nepal and South East Asia, completing his travels in Australia in 1971.

Exhausted by his adventures and aspiring for a higher, spiritual goal in life, he joined ISKCON in May 1972 in Vancouver, Canada. Later that year aged 22 he was very fortunate to become an initiated disciple of His Divine Grace A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami Prabhupāda.

He soon became a book representative for the Bhaktivedanta Book Trust, travelling all over North America, and thus became part of the elite BBT Travelling Airport Sankirtan Party in the early seventies.

In May 1975 he was sent to South Africa to train and encourage the devotees there in book distribution. In the following year, Riddha dāsa spearheaded the first preaching centre with public programmes, at La-Mercy Beach, near Durban.

In 1976 he married Śyāmasundarī dāsī, the first South African Indian disciple of Śrīla Prabhupāda, still a very steadfast devotee. Of their five children born into ISKCON, two are initiated and the remainder are devotees.

In the early nineties, he trained under an elite master of the ancient system of martial arts known as *Hasilkapenu* that teaches the cultivation of a higher consciousness. During that time, he had interests in inner self-development, healing, spiritually-based community lifestyle, and self-sufficiency in the face of global changes.

As an international activist in the mid-nineties, he played a leading role in the fight against religious persecution, taking part in the successful campaign to save the Hare Krishna temple Bhaktivedanta Manor from closure. He was also at that time a special correspondent for *India Link International* (UK), a bi-monthly magazine.

In South Africa he was covered by the press and interviewed on SABC Radio and Radio Lotus, with a focus on religious persecution and injustice.

In 1995 he embarked on writing *Destination South Africa*, and thereafter in 1999 continued writing until 2002 when he completed the updated and extended version, *Mission in the Service of His Divine Grace*.
